Here’s How to Train From Now Through the New Year to Maintain Your Hard-Earned Marathon Fitness

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

A run coach and an exercise physiologist break down the weekly mileage and workouts you need to preserve your speed, strength, and endurance.

You’ve spent months training for your fall marathon, so the thought of losing that hard-earned fitness after race day probably stings. But at the same time, you can’t simply continue logging miles and workouts like you’re in race mode. Trying to stay razor-sharp year-round can lead to injury and burnout; your body needs rest after training for peak fitness.

So, instead, put your marathon fitness on ice. Freeze the adaptations you’ve gained and the endurance you’ve built. Then, you can pick up where you left off on, say, January 1, or whenever it’s time to lock back in for a spring marathon.

If you train right in the weeks between now and the new year, you won’t feel like you’re rebuilding fitness from scratch. Instead, you’ll preserve the key aerobic, muscular, and endurance adaptations you’ve worked so hard to develop.

To do that, we explain the minimum miles you need for maintenance and how speed, strength, and cross-training keep you in prime condition. Expect to return to race training feeling well-rested, confident, and ready to tackle another marathon cycle this spring.

In the first two weeks immediately following your marathon, you’ll want to focus more on rest and easy time-based jogging. Once you’ve recovered, however, you can return to tracking your miles more closely.

The bare minimum mileage you’ll need to maintain will vary from runner to runner, Heather Milton, CSCS, a clinical exercise physiologist and cofounder of Master My Run, tells Runner’s World. Much of it depends on how many miles you regularly put in over the weeks of your marathon training cycle.

“Science does not give us an exact number of what percentage of your marathon volume to decrease to that still allows you to maintain the same amount of aerobic fitness,” Milton explains. From her coaching experience, however, she’s found this number is likely around 50 percent of a runner’s typical weekly mileage.

David Hall, an RRCA- and USATF-certified run coach and founder of Running Coach CT, agrees. When planning his athletes’ schedules for the off-season, he looks at their typical running volume during marathon training and reduces their weekly mileage to no less than half. Every fourth week, however, mileage can drop to as low as 20 percent of their average to help facilitate recovery.

“A runner who was running 60 miles per week during their marathon build could definitely cut that in half,” Milton says. Or, take a runner who averaged around 40 miles per week. This individual could reduce their weekly mileage to 20, add in some cross-training, and expect to maintain much of their progress.

In fact, Milton adds, every runner can benefit from incorporating more of those cross-training sessions in the off-season. “Other forms of exercise can decrease the repetitiveness of the running motion and help you develop more well-rounded fitness, so I do prefer more variety from cross-training in the down-season,” she explains.

At the same time, Milton wants people to do the exercise they enjoy and, of course, runners love running. “I have to be reasonable about what a runner is willing to do and what keeps them healthy while they’re doing it,” she says. If you want to keep running four days per week in the off-season, that’s still workable—with one important caveat: You need to prioritize strength training.

When you’re in the thick of marathon training, running is your main focus. So while you’re still doing strength work during this time, it mostly helps maintain the strength you already have, rather than increase it, Milton explains. The off-season provides an ideal time to really build up and strengthen your muscle mass and bone health as much as possible.

Each week, Milton says to aim for two to three resistance training sessions that focus on heavier weights with a low to moderate number of reps. (You can do more than three weekly sessions as long as you’re not working the same muscle groups two days in a row.) This sets you up for a stronger return to your spring training cycle because it helps improve running economy, your overall neuromuscular health, and your fast-twitch muscle fibers, which help power you through fast bursts of running or hill climbing.

“Plus, when you start training for your next marathon, you’ll be in a stronger position to increase your running volume again because you’ve strengthened all of the areas of your body that were impacted by your previous race, which can help prevent injuries,” Milton adds.

During the off-season, you certainly don’t need to run every day to maintain your fitness. In fact, you can preserve key fitness markers—such as your VO2 max—with as little as three workouts per week.

“The goal is to maintain at least three to four aerobic days per week to maintain your fitness level,” says Milton. You can opt to run as little as once per week, as long as you continue to cross-train on the remaining days, she adds.

Aim for 30 to 60 minutes of whichever aerobic exercise you most enjoy—hit the gym for the elliptical or indoor pool, or get outside for skiing and cycling.

Similarly, the number of running days per week for Hall’s athletes varies, but many have success with three because it allows more time for strength and flexibility work. He also adds a second rest day to the schedules of his runners who typically train six days per week during a marathon block. But in both cases, consistency is crucial—you don’t want to go a week without doing anything. “Do something every other day at a minimum to stay active,” Hall says.

One of your runs each week should be a bit longer than the others, but it doesn’t need to match the lengths of your true marathon long runs. Milton typically recommends a long run between five and 13 miles, depending on the individual’s training volume.

If you don’t want to worry about tracking mileage in the off-season, that’s fine, too. Running for time instead of miles provides the same endurance benefits, Hall says. Around four weeks postmarathon, his athletes will typically start with one-hour long runs. “Then, they’ll go up to 75 minutes, then to 90 minutes, and then maybe up to an hour and 45,” he explains.

Over several weeks, this gradual progression sets his runners up with a solid base to build from when they start their spring marathon training. “But don’t push the pace,” he emphasizes. “These are easy, conversational efforts.”

Whether you run for time or miles, make sure you’re not increasing either too quickly. “Keep the same rules in mind as when you’re building mileage in marathon training,” Milton says. “Aim to increase by no more than 10 percent each week.” Recent research suggests that, to reduce injury risk, you can apply this rule to the distance of any single run, as well.

What does this look like in practice? Let’s say a runner is doing five aerobic sessions per week, which includes about 20 miles of running. Two are cross-training days and the remaining three are running days. They might choose to run three to five miles one day, five to seven miles another day, and then five to 13 miles for their long run. You also don’t need to increase distance as quickly as you would in marathon training, Milton explains.

Over three weeks, she says a runner might balance their mileage as follows:

Week 1: 4 miles, 6 miles, 10 milesWeek 2: 5 miles, 6 miles, 9 milesWeek 3: 3 miles, 7 miles, 11 miles

In the four weeks immediately following your marathon, don’t worry about fast workouts or hitting paces, says Hall. During those first two weeks postrace, he advises his athletes to go for 20- to 30-minute daily walks. In the third and fourth weeks, he reintroduces easy running and a touch of speed.

“The general rule I apply is: no hard running for the number of days that’s equal to the number of miles in the race. So for a marathon, we’re talking about 30 days of walking and conversational effort running—maybe dipping into tempo pace a bit—but certainly nothing above lactate threshold.”

After that time, Hall says runners can start to add back intensity. In fact, he encourages it, suggesting you incorporate either a 20-minute run at threshold pace or an interval session for the same amount of time. He also recommends alternating one mile at your threshold pace, followed by one minute of easy jogging or brisk walking, until you reach 20 minutes.

“Training at lactate threshold pace can make conversational effort feel easier and can help you run faster while still remaining mostly in an aerobic zone,” Hall explains. In other words, your body adapts so running at any pace starts to feel like it requires less work.

Another way to get in some speed during the winter season? Sign up for a holiday race like a Turkey Trot or Jingle Bell Jog, he says. “It can get boring if all you do is train. Racing a 5K keeps the interest alive and lets you put in a hard effort.” Keep in mind, though, you did just run a marathon, so you don’t need to race all out or cross the finish line with a PR.

According to both Hall and Milton, the golden rule that will help you maintain your hard-earned gains is consistency. Once you’ve recovered physically and mentally from 26.2, get back into a routine that keeps you moving. “Doing something is always better than doing nothing,” Hall says—because you can lose 8 percent of your VO2 max in four to six weeks if you pull the plug on activity completely.

Similarly, you’ll want to avoid “stop-and-go” training. “If you’re running two to four days per week, keep running two to four days per week, every week—within reason, because life gets in the way sometimes,” Milton says. But running 30 miles one week, doing nothing for three weeks, and then trying to get back into it can lead to a real struggle, she adds.

As long as you do your best to stay consistent—and don’t stress when the holidays, travel, or other engagements inevitably interfere—you’ll find your fitness quite close to where you left it after your fall marathon. All that’s left for you to do? Thaw it out with a fresh marathon training cycle this spring.

Morgan is a gear editor who has been with Runner’s World since 2017. She started as an intern ghostwriting The Warmup, a bygone version of the daily RW newsletter. Now, she tests and reviews anything you might find on runners’ feet-from crew socks and compression boots to carbon-plated super shoes.

A lifelong runner and shoe geek, Morgan has been chasing the perfect pair of kicks since she joined her grade school cross-country team. Since then, she ran as a Division I walk-on for the cross-country and track & field teams at the University of Delaware, where she studied English and Biology. She has one full marathon under her belt, and has raced more halfs and 5Ks than she can count.

Skip Salmon: 15 More Sustainable Fish Alternatives

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

From Japan to New Jersey, salmon is one of the most in-demand, beloved fish in the world, prized for its rich flavor and succulently flaky flesh. But while it's undeniably delicious, there can be some nuances in how sustainable certain varieties are. Sourcing sustainable alternatives can be tricky, but knowing the right certifications and sources can go a long way toward purchasing the right seafood.

Salmon isn't inherently unsustainable, but an incredible amount of global demand can lead to some less-than-ideal practices. In particular, open-net salmon farms may cause environmental issues, whether that's pollution runoff, unsustainable sources of fish feed, or the spread of diseases and parasites to wild populations. One of the biggest differences between farmed and wild salmon is that wild varieties are often more sustainable, depending on the fishery, and well-managed populations are carefully monitored to ensure long-term viability. Still, New Zealand is famous for its highly rated salmon aquaculture, so it would be wrong to paint all farms with the same brush.

When it comes to sourcing other, more sustainable fish, it's often a solid idea to look for something small and fast-growing rather than something large and predatory. While some certifications may greenwash the sustainability of seafood, guidance from the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration can help consumers identify responsibly managed fisheries. Despite salmon's unique qualities, there are many fish out there that are more affordable and sustainable and can still be high-quality substitutes in your regular diet.

If you don't want to change up textures and taste, char can be a more sustainable fish with a mild flavor and fewer calories. As a salmonid, it's remarkably similar to salmon and has the same pink flesh, marbling, and savory skin you've already come to love.

Few fish species are quite as environmentally friendly as responsibly sourced sardines, since they're small prey fish that grow quite rapidly in their first years of life. Fresh, dried, frozen, or tinned, sardines come in enough varieties that there's an option for just about everyone.

Freshwater fish get a bad rap for being milder and leaner than ocean-dwelling species, and responsibly farmed rainbow trout is about as tasty and environmentally friendly as it gets. A thick, skin-on fillet cooked with a pat of butter can be just as moist and succulent, and modern farming practices can be quite eco-conscious.

Tasting like someone managed to cross the buttery sweetness of lobster with a fish, sablefish, also known as black cod, is an ocean delicacy. Even well-managed farming operations can have a relatively low environmental impact, particularly when they use responsible feed sources and strict measures to prevent disease.

Essentially just a giant perch, barramundi is a flaky, versatile protein you can prepare exactly the same way you'd cook salmon. They're a unique case in that they're quite large but can thrive on feeds containing plant-based ingredients, so responsibly managed commercial farming operations can have a relatively low environmental impact.

A catfish may not be much to look at, but they're certainly a lot to taste. As bottom-feeders, they can thrive on an omnivorous diet that includes plant-based ingredients, and while they might be a touch lean, few proteins are quite as good when deep-fried or bathed in butter.

One of the mildest fish you can use in just about anything, halibut is a largely sustainable choice, particularly when it's wild-caught in the Pacific. Atlantic populations have faced steep declines from overfishing, so be sure to source it appropriately.

When you're looking for something to bread, batter, or mix into a salad, few types of seafood are quite as good as whiting. Like other fish, however, its sustainability depends on its source, and some well-managed fisheries in U.S. Atlantic waters are more sustainable than those targeting depleted populations elsewhere.

While mahi-mahi sourced from Central America and southern Asia can be a bit problematic, those caught in responsibly managed fisheries in the Atlantic can be quite sustainable. While the fish can reach a respectable 3 feet in length, they grow rapidly, and their populations can recover quite quickly with responsible management.

Snapper is incredibly delicious, but of all the various species, yellowtail snapper from well-managed fisheries can be a particularly responsible choice. Historically, government authorities and local fishers work together to manage certain populations, helping ensure that these fish are harvested responsibly.

Huge, sweet, and oh-so-fun to reel in, the greater amberjack is a common sight for saltwater fishers looking to make tacos or cut thick steaks for the grill. Strict controls over harvesting wild-caught fish can help protect vulnerable populations, although sustainability varies by fishery, so it's worth checking the source before buying it at a seafood counter.

Sometimes called sunshine bass, this hybrid fish is a cross between a saltwater striped bass and a freshwater white bass. These fish grow quickly and tolerate a wide range of conditions, making it relatively easy to raise them sustainably with minimal environmental impact.

Eating carp can not only be sustainable, but buying invasive varieties sourced from the American South can actually be a boon for the environment! Several species of invasive carp have spread through U.S. waterways, so every bite of their earthy, slightly tangy flesh can help support efforts to protect these delicate ecosystems.

Blackened red drum, also called redfish, was once so popular that it prompted an outright commercial fishing ban in federal waters of the Gulf of Mexico. While responsibly farm-raised red drum can still be a sustainable choice, along with certain wild-caught black drum, it's generally still best to avoid wild-caught red drum, as sustainability varies by fishery.

Fresh, clean, and a premium delicacy, grouper can be a gray area of sustainability. While many species, like goliath, Nassau, and Gulf groupers, face serious conservation concerns and may be illegal to harvest in certain areas, responsibly sourced black grouper can be a great substitute for premium-grade salmon.

“Scot Will Be There” Parker’s First West Ham Decisions Could Come Within Hours

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Claret & Hugh has been informed within the past few moments that incoming West Ham manager Scott Parker is likely to have some input into team selection for tomorrow night’s Championship game against Cardiff City. The former Hammers midfielder is also expected to attend the match, despite Mark Noble being set to take charge on the touchline.

As West Ham confirmed in their official statement earlier today, Noble, Steve Potts and Gerard Prenderville have been placed in temporary charge of preparations following the dismissal of Nuno Espírito Santo. However, the information we’ve received suggests that Parker could still have a say in the team that faces Cardiff.

A C&H source told us just moments ago: “Scot will be there and he’ll have some influence on the team even if he can’t do all of it.”

West Ham’s final training session ahead of the match took place earlier today at Rush Green, before Parker had even signed his proposed two-and-a-half-year contract. But that doesn’t mean he will have no input at all, particularly with the new manager expected to be in attendance tomorrow evening.

Being something of a Championship specialist, and of course a former Hammer, Parker will already be familiar with West Ham’s form and performances this season. I suspect he’ll also have a reasonable idea of which players he wants in the team and which ones might benefit from a spell on the sidelines.

As Claret & Hugh revealed earlier today, Parker’s proposed contract is closely tied to West Ham securing promotion this season. With that in mind, there is every reason for him to want to influence proceedings as quickly as possible, even if Noble is the man officially overseeing tomorrow’s game.

And, to be perfectly honest, I don’t think Parker needs to reinvent the wheel to make some immediate improvements.

There are a couple of really easy wins available to the new Irons gaffer, and some of them should be blindingly obvious to anybody who has watched West Ham over the past few months.

Morato was Nuno’s first choice left back.

Firstly, a rousing team talk and a little bit of urgency might be enough to address West Ham’s dreadful starts to games. It was a recurring problem under Nuno Espírito Santo, and one the former manager never seemed capable of fixing. Getting the players motivated and ready to compete from the opening whistle would be a welcome change.

Then there’s the fairly straightforward business of putting round pegs in round holes. For starters, let’s play an actual left-back in the left-back position. That alone would immediately improve the balance of the team rather than continuing to ask players to operate in unfamiliar roles.

Finding the right blend in midfield would also be of huge benefit. West Ham have spent far too much time surrendering control of what is arguably the most important area of the pitch, despite having some talented options available. Getting that balance right could make an enormous difference to the team’s performances.

And finally, there’s Jarrod Bowen. Surely one of the most obvious priorities for any incoming West Ham manager is getting our best attacking player into as many goalscoring positions as possible. Bowen has spent far too much time being asked to produce something out of nothing, and a better-functioning team should make his job considerably easier.

Okay, it’s all a bit paint by numbers, and there’s nothing particularly revolutionary or overly tactical about those suggestions. But that’s rather the point.

West Ham’s problems have often appeared so obvious that you wonder why they’ve been allowed to continue for so long. Parker doesn’t necessarily need weeks on the training ground to recognise them, and even a quick word with the players before kick-off might help address some of the most glaring deficiencies.

Of course, Noble will be responsible for managing the team against Cardiff, and there’s only so much Parker can realistically influence before he’s properly taken charge.

But if the new manager can offer a few sensible suggestions and inject a little urgency into proceedings, I’d suggest that would already be a step in the right direction.

Kris Gonzo (commonly known to the West Ham community as Gonzo) is the co-founder of Hammers Chat (established 2014) and the lead video content creator and columnist for Claret and Hugh.

With over a decade of professional sports media experience, he is a frequent West Ham United analyst and contributor for Sky Sports News, BBC Football Focus, and BBC Radio 5 Live. He previously served as an official video and content creator for West Ham United FC during the club's tenure at Upton Park and featured in the official documentary commemorating the historic final game at the Boleyn Ground.

A lifelong Hammer since the legendary 10-0 victory over Bury, when he isn't providing daily match analysis on the Hammers Chat YouTube Channel or broadcasting on X (@GONZObignose), he can be found walking his dog and restoring classic pinball machines.

One developer has been waiting for weeks on approval for a game it planned to release today.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Many indie game developers have recently posted on social media about unexpectedly long approval wait times for their game submissions on Steam, with some of the approvals causing developers to miss their planned launch dates.

For example, Studio Daimon, the team behind an upcoming title called Food Devils, had planned to launch the game in early access today after submitting its build on October 12th. But the game is “still awaiting approval,” according to a post from game director Alfred Kamon. “Even now, we don’t know when Steam will give us the final OK to finally release our game. We sent another ticket last week saying that the situation was urgent and that we might miss our release date because of this, but it also got ignored.”

In October, publisher 2 Left Thumbs said it missed its planned launch of Wheel Make It “by nearly two weeks” due to the long wait for build reviews. You can read some details about the studio’s setbacks, including failing a review for a “one single option in the menu” not being accessible with a controller, in a Steam post. There are lots of other recent posts about review build waits as well.

Valve says on a Steam developer website that a review of a game’s store presence “typically takes 3-5 business days to complete,” though the company suggests submitting a page for review “at least 7 business days before you want it live to account for potential changes you’ll need to make.”

However, as of late, Steam has seemingly been grappling with a sharp increase in games; October’s Steam Next Fest had 7,240 games, a 65.2 percent increase over June’s Steam Next Fest, according to How to Market a Game. A Reddit user also found that 49.9 percent of all games released since Valve started asking developers in 2024 to label games that use AI now have an AI disclosure.

Kamon and 2 Left Thumbs suspect the longer waits are due to “slop” slowing down the process. “We can only assume it’s because Steam’s review queue is clogged up by AI slop, but the whole process is now taking several weeks for some developers,” said Kamon.

In a new post from Valve published after we first ran this story, the company acknowledged that following a “a significant increase in submissions for store and page review in recent months,” it’s “running a little behind right now” and taking “up to 12 business days” for reviews, saying that much of the delay is “having to re-review games.” It has updated its release checklist for developers, including requiring them to run a game through Steam for at least five minutes. Valve also says that further improvements are on the way for the build review process.

Itch.io has also been dealing with a rise in games, with the platform saying last weekend that “The number of game pages being created per-day has doubled in the past 2 months.” Last night, the platform said it “Just deleted a couple terabytes of ai slop 😌”

Update, November 3rd: Added new post from Valve.

Apple Is Putting an Age Limit on Falling in Love With AI

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Dating and AI companion apps have new age gate restrictions, while apps that share your location will have to disclose it.

Practicing flirting using Replika's AI system.

Falling in love with artificial intelligence raises a lot of questions about loneliness, human connection and what the heck we’re doing as a species. Apple has a more immediate concern: Are you at least 18?

The company is tightening its App Store rules, requiring dating apps and AI companions that offer romantic or sexual interactions to carry an 18-and-over age rating. The change applies whether you’re looking for love with another human being or flirting with a chatbot that never leaves you on read.

In a notice to developers on Monday, Apple said it will introduce two new content descriptors on Nov. 16: “Dating and Relationships” and “Shared Location.” Developers will have to disclose whether their apps offer dating features, including romantic or sexual interactions with virtual companions, or whether they allow users to share their real-world locations with others.

Location-sharing features may also affect age ratings, depending on the country. In Brazil, for example, apps that allow users to share their locations must carry a minimum age rating of 16, while dating apps require an 18-and-over rating. Developers have until Jan. 12, 2027, to complete the new disclosures for all their apps.

Most major dating apps, including Tinder and Hinge, already require users to be at least old enough to vote (in the US, that is). The more interesting part is how Apple’s rules could affect AI companions, which have become increasingly popular.

Last year, Character.AI, a platform where users can create and interact with fictional AI personalities, removed open-ended chats for users under 18. The company continued offering other features to younger users, but the decision came amid growing concerns about the emotional and psychological risks of forming relationships with AI.

A 2026 Common Sense Media report, which surveyed more than 1,000 children ages 9 to 17, found that many were already treating AI as a confidant. An earlier 2025 study found that nearly three-quarters of teens had used AI companions, with a third choosing chatbots over humans for serious conversations. The nonprofit has warned that such relationships can pose risks to minors, particularly around emotional attachment and dependency.

Apple’s announcement also follows the company’s expanded parental controls in iOS 27, which gave parents more control over their children’s apps, browsing and screen time. The update introduced features that let parents approve websites before their children visit them, set time limits for app categories and restrict access to potentially harmful content.

However, an “18-plus” rating doesn’t automatically mean everyone has to verify their age. The labels work with parental restrictions, while Apple may require users to confirm they’re adults before downloading those adult apps in certain regions.

So the new rules won’t necessarily stop teenagers from finding a digital soulmate. They should, however, make it harder for apps offering romantic AI relationships to slip past age-based content restrictions — regardless of whether a chatbot says you’re mature for your age.

Dodge Confirms Return to the NASCAR Cup Series in 2027

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The brand used the 2026 SEMA Show to announce it’s completing its fast-track return to NASCAR with Kaulig Racing.

On Monday night at the 2026 SEMA Show, Dodge announced its long-awaited return to the NASCAR Cup Series after a 14-year absence.

This move has been anticipated since its Stellantis-partnered truck brand, Ram, returned to the Craftsman Truck Series this year. With it, Kaulig Racing—which has been operating its five-truck Ram team, will be the first to move over to Dodge.

Dodge’s return is being fast-tracked, moving into the sport after two generations removed. But to Stellantis North America head of American brands, North America marketing and retail strategy Tim Kuniskis, this approach is the only way. As Dodge looks to recenter “gasoline-powered performance,” as its North Star, the only stage truly suitable is the top level of NASCAR.

“Everyone said it was impossible to get back in one year. We said, 'Watch this,'" said Kuniskis. "Sure, we could spend three years planning, testing and perfecting ... or we could do all three in real time, on the track. It's going to be exponentially harder this way. But slow has never been part of Dodge DNA. LFG."

When Dodge last raced at the Cup Series level, it was the end of the Generation 5 era with a race car wearing the Dodge Charger’s face. The Cup car has changed drastically since then through two generations, running the 7 Gen or ’Next Gen’ car since 2022.

The driver announcement is set for this weekend during NASCAR Championship Weekend at Homestead-Miami. Currently, Kaulig Racing runs Ty Dillon in the number 10 and AJ Allmendinger in the No. 16, and the driver lineup is not expected to change. The only wrench thrown into the mix is the unexpected availability of Brad Keselowski, who left RFK—a team he owns 20 percent of—due to disagreements about his future role with the company and his desire to keep racing as long as possible.

Keselowski and Dodge have a special bond, with Keselowski delivering Dodge and Penske Racing a championship in 2012, their farewell season in the sport. “I would say everything's on the table,” Keselowski said during his press conference at Martinsville last weekend. “I have not had a discussion with Dodge or Ram. I'm certainly very open-minded to it alongside all of the OEMs. I haven't given up on a relationship with Ford in the future as well, along with the other two OEMs of Chevrolet and Toyota. Everything's on the table right now for me.”

Kaulig Racing team owner Matt Kaulig founded the team in 2016, four seasons after Dodge first left the sport in the Xfinity (now O’Reilly) Series and then in 2020 at the Cup level with Chevrolet. “This is a very exciting moment for both Chris Rice and me. We launched Kaulig Racing a decade ago and have overcome so many challenges together, which makes it even more remarkable to be announced as the anchor team for Dodge’s return to the NASCAR Cup Series. What a monumental time to be a member of Kaulig Racing,” he said.

Dodge will have a major presence this year at the Snowball Derby, the premier off-season 300-lap pavement super late model race in Pensacola, Florida, held every December. Their new Next Gen NASCAR Cup Car—presumably Charger-branded and inspired—will be revealed on Friday, December 4th. Later that weekend, the brand will also host the Dodge Sno-Brawl, featuring celebrity drivers racing in Dodge Charger Late Models.

Victoria Beaver is a nomadic sports writer who spends her time hopping between race tracks and hippie farms. She’s covered every corner of motorsports that will let her in from 410 Sprints to NASCAR to Supercross. Her daily driver is a 2010 Subaru that she refused to do the smallest amount of preventative maintenance on. Instead, she spends her free time and money building a 42-foot Skoolie to one day travel the country full time.

JetBlue Raises Bag Fees Again—One Checked Bag Can Cost $140 Roundtrip, Other Airlines Likely To Follow

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

JetBlue has raised checked bag fees again. Your first bag now costs at least $50 each way on domestic flights, and as much as $70 if you pay within 24 hours of departure on a peak travel date. That’s $140 roundtrip for one bag. They already raised bag fees earlier this year. And JetBlue has repeatedly gone first with these increases, then other airlines follow. So even if you don’t fly JetBlue, their tests of how much more passengers will pay can wind up costing you. A First Bag Starts At $50. A Second Reaches $85. The new pricing applies to bookings made November 3 onward. Here’s the new domestic schedule, per bag, each way: Checked bag Paid more than 24 hours ahead Paid within 24 hours First bag, off-peak $50 $60 First bag, peak $55 $70 Second bag, off-peak $70 $75 Second bag, peak $75 $85 Peak pricing is November 19–30, December 17–January 18, and May 20–September 8 next summer. A family of four checking one bag each, without a waiver, pays $440 roundtrip on peak dates even when they pay early. Wait until check-in and it’s $560. On transatlantic Main Base and EvenMore Base fares, the first bag is $80 off-peak or $90 peak when prepaid, rising to $90 or $100 within 24 hours. Standard Main and EvenMore transatlantic fares still include a first bag. Mosaic 1 elite status includes one checked bag and Mosaic 2–4 include two. JetBlue Plus and Premier cardholders have a first checked bag benefit. Those exemptions become a pitch for their credit card since things are worse for everyone else. And while they give you a discount for paying in advance, bag fees are refundable only when the entire booking is canceled before departure. If you pay for an extra bag you don’t wind up checking, you’re out of luck. By putting fear of high prices in passengers, they may generate revenue for services the customer winds up not ever needing and using. JetBlue Keeps Leading The Industry Toward Higher Bag Fees When I covered JetBlue’s increase in March, their cheapest first bag went from $35 to $39. The peak advance price rose to $49. Today’s $50 minimum is 28% higher than that March minimum, and 43% higher than the $35 price before it. In 2018, JetBlue led the move to a $30 first checked bag. United followed that same week. In January 2020, JetBlue went to $35 unless you prepaid. United announced its matching move the next month. In 2024, JetBlue added peak and off-peak baggage pricing, after already raising fees earlier that year. This spring, JetBlue raised fees in late March. United announced another increase days later. JetBlue isn’t the consumer-first airline anymore. When they introduced bag fees in 2015, the first bag was $20 online or at a kiosk, $25 at the counter. They defended their direction by pointing to how much worse other airlines were, declaring: The world is going in a very dramatic direction toward more and more commoditization. That’s not where we’re going. Now they’re leading in the race to the bottom. The Government Encourages This JetBlue tied the spring increase to rising operating costs. But an airline’s bag fee isn’t derived from the fuel needed to carry the bag. Fares and fees are set according to what airlines think customers will pay, subject to competition. Higher bag fees let JetBlue raise the cost of a trip while leaving the advertised airfare alone. They also make it harder to compare the total price across airlines. And the government rewards them for doing it. Domestic air transportation is generally subject to a 7.5% federal excise tax. Separate baggage fees are excluded from that tax. Every dollar an airline moves out of fares and into fees saves them 7.5 cents, even if the total price remains the same. I’ve made this point through previous rounds of JetBlue increases: anyone serious about pushing back on bag fees should start with the tax preference that encourages airlines to move money out of fares and into baggage charges. AMD needs a big quarter to escape Intel’s fate as AI fears grow

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

AMD's shares are still up more than 120% this year, but investors are punishing any sign of weakness.

AMD reports third-quarter results on Tuesday after its shares fell about 17% in two days alongside Intel. Analysts expect revenue to rise 41%, but investors say AMD needs a big beat and a strong outlook as AI spending fears grow.

AMD needs a strong quarter and a bold forecast when it reports results after the US market closes on Tuesday, investors told Bloomberg.

“A modest report just won’t cut it,” said Neville Javeri, a portfolio manager at Allspring Global Investments, which owns the stock.

The pressure comes from Intel. Its shares had their worst day since at least 1982 on Friday after a weak outlook. AMD fell 10% the same day and another 7.5% on Monday, though it is still up more than 120% this year.

Analysts expect AMD’s third-quarter earnings to jump 92% on a 41% rise in revenue. AMD had guided for about $13B in sales, after a record second quarter.

Part of the hope rests on processors. AI agents lean heavily on CPUs, as AMD’s chief technology officer argued in July, and the success of Meta’s Muse agents has led some analysts to raise their targets. Meta is AMD’s second-largest customer, and AMD’s Helios AI racks are now being tested there.

The risk is the mood around AI. Big spenders such as Alphabet and Meta have been punished for spending that eats into their cash, raising fears of a pullback that would hit chipmakers. In July, TSMC’s shares fell despite record revenue.

“I’m just not super comfortable with the valuation,” said Jim Worden of the Wealth Consulting Group, who has sold his AMD shares.

Finance of America grows origination volume but reports $110M loss in Q3

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Finance of America reported a $110M net loss in Q3 2026, driven mainly by $125M in noncash fair value adjustments. Funded volume rose 24% year over year to $748M, while adjusted net income was $6M and adjusted EBITDA was $17M.   AI Summary

Finance of America (FOA) reported a net loss of $110 million in the third quarter of 2026, as noncash fair value adjustments weighed on results despite growth in the company’s home equity lending business.

The Plano, Texas-based company on Tuesday reported $748 million in funded volume for the quarter ending Sept. 30, up 24% from $603 million during the same period last year. Year-to-date funded volume reached $2.07 billion, a 17% increase from the first nine months of 2025.

FOA recorded a basic loss of $5.24 per share, compared with a loss of 98 cents per share in the third quarter of 2025. The quarterly net loss was primarily driven by $125 million in noncash fair value adjustments.

On an adjusted basis, FOA reported net income of $6 million, or 28 cents per share, compared with $33 million, or $1.33 per share, a year earlier. Adjusted EBITDA totaled $17 million, down from $55 million in the third quarter of 2025.

“The third quarter of 2026 demonstrated the continued strength of our platform, with funded origination volume growing 24% year over year. While higher interest rates drove near-term pressure on earnings, we believe this impact is temporary,” CEO Graham Fleming said in a statement. “We have already taken steps to improve margins.”

During the company’s earnings call, Fleming said the company remains focused on production growth, operational efficiency and cash generation, and explained that margins compressed after the Federal Reserve shifted from cutting to raising interest rates.

“We have already made the necessary changes to product pricing, and we expect margins to return to historical levels in the first half of 2027,” Fleming told investors.

“Finance of America has grown volumes on average by 20% each year since 2023. The third quarter was a challenging rate environment, with the 10-year Treasury yields increasing over 75 basis points during the quarter, a reaction to the Fed’s pivot to raising rates and elevated inflation,” he added.

FOA’s retirement solutions segment, which originates home equity-based financing products, including reverse mortgages, recorded $748 million in funded volume during the quarter, up 24% year over year.

“Our retirement solutions revenue margin was impacted in the third quarter, decreasing to 9%, compared with 11.3% in the third quarter of 2025, and a historical average since the start of 2024 of 10.7%,” Fleming said during the call. “We believe these impacts are temporary and do not change the underlying fundamentals of our business.”

Revenue for the segment totaled $67 million, down 1% from the prior-year period and a decrease from Q2 2026‘s $74 million. Pretax income fell to $3 million, down from $10 million a year earlier, while adjusted net income declined to $9 million, down from $20 million.

The company attributed the decline in profitability primarily to pressure on origination margins as benchmark interest rates increased during the quarter. It said it implemented product and pricing adjustments to address the impact and expects margins to return to historical levels.

For the first nine months of 2026, the retirement solutions segment generated $2.07 billion in funded volume, up 17% year over year. Revenue increased 14% to $208 million.

“Since 2023, our submission volumes have increased by 21% annually, while our funded volume has grown by 20% annually. This is across our product suite, as HECM volume is up 14% year over year, HomeSafe first liens are up 15%, and HomeSafe seconds are up 63%,” FOA President Kristen Sieffert said during the call.

FOA’s portfolio management segment, which generates revenue through net interest income and fair value changes on portfolio assets, reported a pretax loss of $138 million, compared with an $11 million loss in the third quarter of 2025.

The company attributed the loss primarily to negative non-cash fair value adjustments on retained interests in securitizations. Portfolio management reported adjusted net income of $11 million, down from $30 million a year earlier.

Assets under management in the segment increased 23% year over year to $37.4 billion. The growth reflected new originations and the acquisition of Onity Mortgage Corp.‘s HECM servicing portfolio on July 1.

For the first nine months of 2026, portfolio management recorded a pretax loss of $128 million, compared with pretax income of $202 million in the same period last year. Adjusted net income totaled $57 million, down from $66 million.

FOA ended the third quarter with $118 million in cash and cash equivalents, up from $85 million at the end of June. The company attributed the increase to cash generation from originations and capital markets activity.

Total equity stood at $307 million as of Sept. 30, including $259 million attributable to common stock. Book value was $28.08 per common share.

The company said it expects to complete a $60 million paydown of senior secured notes in November. Looking ahead to the final quarter of 2026, chief financial officer Matt Engel said the company expects funded volume to reach $3.1 billion to $3.4 billion, a range “supported by consumer demographics, our product breadth, and ongoing conversion improvements.”

I've Had This Modular Sofa for Four Years, and Its Secret Twin "Bed" Is a Hosting Game-Changer

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The first couch I ordered for my basement was a bust. No matter how hard they tried, the delivery men couldn’t angle it to get it down the narrow stairs. Because I live in a 1930s home, all the charm turns to chaos when it comes to fitting new furniture. I was literally stuck because a family of five was visiting for the holidays and they had nothing to sit (or sleep) on. Plus, what even is a basement without a comfy couch?

Ideally, I wanted a sleeper sofa to accommodate my guests and let my kids have sleepovers. But there was absolutely no way I would risk buying another couch that couldn’t come apart and was stuck in a corner. For round two, I knew that I needed a modular sectional. After a bit of research, I came across the Burrow Range sectional and was elated that it could be assembled like a building-block puzzle that can grow over time if I wanted to add more sections.

Since the basement isn’t huge, my selection was a four-piece model that included two arm pieces as well as an ottoman. Because my dreams of a sleeper were no more, I liked that the Range has flippable top cushions that, when pulled back, could make a twin-size bed. Happily, four years later, the sectional has survived many, many sleepovers and now resides in my bedroom. Read on to see how it transitioned from child-hopping to a peaceful nook two floors up.

Best For: A growing household or someone who likes to change the layout of their space often.

Not ideal for: Someone who is very tall. If you are taller than 6 feet, the sofa may sit too low for you.

Price at review (4-piece sectional lounger): $1,271 (originally $2,119)

Bottom Line: The modular sectional is comfortable and won’t sag or end up with flat cushions, even after years of use.

Because I needed the sectional before the winter holidays, I was glad that I could order by early December and still get my sofa before Santa came to town. The four big boxes for the cushions and a little box for legs came to my front door within a week.

My husband and I brought down all the boxes to the basement where we started to unbox and assemble. The overall process took no more than 30 minutes. In a nutshell, you need to line up the holes with the metal braces and secure those with large plastic knob screws. The cushions have two strips of Velcro that stick to the black fabric at the base to keep them from sliding out from beneath.

The sofa pieces are easy to secure together.

Compared to my living room couch, which is a mid-century modern style West Elm reversible sectional, the Burrow has a bit more bounce to the cushion. While the seats are softer, I would still describe it as medium-firm. As soon as I sit down, it feels relaxing. Because it is only 28 inches high, it is a little lower than I am used to. (This still works out because I can slouch down and stretch my feet up on the ottoman when I watch a movie.)

Later, when I moved the sectional up to my bedroom, I lie across all three seat cushions, with my head resting on the arm cushion, as if I am reading in bed. The arm cushions are slightly angled, with more padding at the bottom to create a gentle incline that supports my head.

I chose the Stone Gray upholstery, an almost salt-and-pepper look, in performance flatweave. This is my third woven upholstery sofa, and it is the softest. It feels similar to a worn-in tweed jacket. I think it actually got a bit softer with age. I am happy to report there is absolutely no pilling or loose threads.

My biggest pet peeve is saggy couch cushions. I cannot stand it when the fabric looks crumpled on the seats, and there are sloppy valleys on the back cushions. After having this sectional for four years, I can vouch that it still looks the same as the day we assembled it. Which is a small miracle because when the Range lived in the basement, it was base camp for elementary-age boys playing video games and jumping around. If for some reason you wanted to fluff, you can unzip the underside of the back cushions and take out the filling to give it more shape.

In terms of stains and spills, if any kids spilled something on the sofa, I don’t know about it because it has no markings. Come to think of it, I don’t think I’ve ever had to clean the sofa beyond brushing off crumbs.

I did catch my cat trying to stretch (scratch) on the back of the sectional a few times, but I shooed him away, and he did not make it a habit. When I checked, there were never any loops or snags. (I cannot say the same about another woven sofa that is a victim of his claws.)

The cushions are still in terrific shape.

The flexibility of the Burrow Range sectional is everything. That’s the main reason I would highly recommend this couch. Not only do the pieces come in individual boxes that can fit through narrow doorways of older homes, but you can customize the sectional to your exact needs.

Originally, I needed something to help with hosting my out-of-town family, but this sectional has proven again and again that it’s a high-quality piece that can hold up for years to come. I still can’t believe that the cushions are in tip-top shape.

Pricewise, it’s a solid buy for mid-range furniture, especially because I don’t see a reason to replace it any time soon. Even when I ended up getting a new green modular sectional to go with the new wallpaper that I installed during my basement makeover, I found a spot for it in my bedroom. The Range now serves as a reading nook or a makeshift WFH station when I don’t want to stay in bed but feel too lazy to walk down the stairs to my home office. I added a Häti pouf with a scalloped EtúHome board next to the Burrow ottoman, and it serves as a perch for my coffee.

All in all, I am very happy with this purchase. It is durable, comfortable, and boasts clean lines and a silhouette that is approachable, but a bit more interesting than a standard traditional sofa.

Michael Carrick's fate at Manchester United is inevitable – it's finally time for Antonio Conte to take his position in the Old Trafford home dugout

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

It's increasingly likely that the Red Devils will be on the lookout for yet another new manager soon

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Michael Carrick's struggles mean Manchester United are expected to soon be looking for their ninth manager of the post-Alex Ferguson era. To prevent further drift and decay, it is essential the club gets the next appointment right. It is time to hand the reins to... Antonio Conte.

Conte has been linked with the job before. He was a candidate following Jose Mourinho’s sacking, but United ended up sticking with Ole Gunnar Solskjaer after an impressive spell as interim boss. When Solskjaer himself came under pressure in the autumn of 2021, reports in Italy indicated Conte was interested in replacing him. But the call never came.

United were supposedly deterred by Conte’s combative nature. They feared he was a short-term option rather than a manager who would rebuild the club over several years. If United were to let those same concerns rule Conte out as Carrick's successor, they would be making a grave mistake.

Granted, Conte is unlikely to build a dynasty at Old Trafford. Since leaving Juventus in 2014, he has not completed more than two seasons with any club. And there is no doubt he is combustible. Conte is more kick-up-the-backside than arm-around-the-shoulder and not everyone in the United squad would respond well to his methods.

Still, Conte’s track record speaks for itself. He has won six league titles with four different teams. In his last five club roles, he has only failed to scoop silverware at Tottenham Hotspur. Even there, Conte brought about an initial upturn, guiding Spurs into the Champions League at Arsenal’s expense in 2021/22.

It is not as if he has only ever taken over teams on the verge of success. With United surely to soon be looking for another man to revive a fallen giant, they should note that Conte led Juventus to their first Serie A success post-Calciopoli in 2012. Both Chelsea and Napoli finished 10th the year before Conte’s arrival; he won the league in his debut campaign at both clubs. At Inter, Conte ended an 11-year wait for a scudetto in 2021.

United cannot afford to be too precious about Conte’s tendency to move on quickly. Their recent managerial picks have only lasted one or two years anyway. At least Conte would give the club a better chance of success during his own short stay.

The best features, fun and footballing quizzes, straight to your inbox every week.

Conte’s reputation for putting noses out of joint would be no bad thing for United, a sleeping giant in need of a rude awakening. The Italian has the force of personality to drag the club out of its malaise. It is certainly hard to imagine players being openly critical of his tactics, as Bruno Fernandes was of Carrick’s after the defeat by Como. Conte would command instant respect in the dressing room.

Of course, there is no guarantee Conte would succeed at Old Trafford. United have tried virtually everything since Ferguson retired, going back to the drawing board more often than an overworked town planner. The club’s problems run much deeper than the manager. Would anyone really be surprised if they were in the exact same position in two or three years’ time?

Failure is not inevitable, though, and despite the understandable doom and gloom, United’s squad does contain talent. Conte would quickly make them more competitive, and morale would rise with improving results. This season is not a write-off yet. But if United want to salvage something from it, there is only one man they need to call.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/payers/medicare-part-d-premiums-projected-rise-50-2027-without-plan-switching-kff" hreflang="en">Medicare Part D premiums projected to rise 50% in 2027 without plan switching: KFF</a>

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →
Medicare Part D premiums projected to rise 50% in 2027 without plan switching: KFF" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Medicare beneficiaries who remain in their current stand-alone Part D drug plans could face sharply higher premiums in 2027 if they don’t switch to lower-cost plans during open enrollment, according to a KFF analysis.

The average monthly premium for stand-alone prescription drug plans, or PDPs, is projected to rise 50%, from $34 in 2026 to $51 in 2027, if current enrollees remain in the same plans, according to the analysis from KFF, an independent source for health policy research, polling and news.

The increase would widen the gap between stand-alone drug plans and Medicare Advantage plans that include prescription drug coverage. KFF projects that drug coverage through Medicare Advantage plans that offer Part D coverage, or MA-PDs, will average $9 a month in 2027, compared with $51 for PDPs. The average PDP premium would rise by $17, compared with an increase of less than $2 for MA-PDs.

For Medicare Part D prescription drug coverage, the average beneficiary has 28 Medicare Advantage prescription drug plans (MA-PDs) and 9 stand-alone prescription drug plans (PDPs) to choose from, with varying costs associated with these different options. KFF analysts examined premiums and cost-sharing requirements for Part D coverage in 2027 and how these costs have changed over time, based on Medicare plan landscape and benefits data from the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS).

The $51 figure assumes current PDP enrollees remain in their existing plans. The actual average premium could be lower as beneficiaries switch to less expensive plans during open enrollment and new beneficiaries select coverage.

CMS projected a much smaller increase in the national average Part D premium, from $35 to $36. KFF noted that CMS, which based its estimate on September 2026 enrollment, did not explain its calculation methodology.

Among PDP enrollees who do not receive low-income subsidies, 76%, or 10.4 million people, would pay higher premiums if they keep their current plans. About 2.4 million would see their monthly premiums increase by more than $50.

That's a sharp increase from 2026, when only 38,000 enrollees faced monthly premium increases exceeding $50, according to KFF. The Trump administration’s decision to discontinue the Part D Premium Stabilization Demonstration after 2026 ended the additional premium subsidies that were provided to PDP sponsors and the cap on the maximum allowable monthly PDP premium increase, which was set at $50 for 2026, according to KFF analysts.

Another 4.1 million beneficiaries without low-income subsidies are enrolled in zero-premium PDPs this year, but no zero-premium PDPs will be available in 2027.

“With some plans increasing premiums substantially, the stakes are higher this year for people with Medicare stand-alone drug coverage if they sit on the sidelines during open enrollment,” Juliette Cubanski, a vice president and director of the Program on Medicare Policy at KFF, said in a statement. “There are relatively low-premium PDP options, but people facing large premium increases need to take action if they want to avoid paying significantly more.”

According to KFF's analysis, if PDP enrollees who are not receiving Part D Low-Income Subsidies (LIS) keep their current plan in 2027, more than half (55%), or 7.5 million people, will pay premiums of less than $20 per month, but nearly 30%, or 3.9 million people, will pay at least $100 per month.

Average monthly premiums for the eight national PDPs are projected to range from around $6 to $204 in 2027. Among national PDPs, the two lowest-premium plans are Centene’s Wellcare Value Script Rx, an enhanced PDP with an average premium of $6.50 for 2027, up from $5.80 in 2026,  and Wellcare Classic, a basic PDP with an average premium of $10.90 for 2027, up from $8.60 in 2026, according to the analysis. The national PDPs with the highest average monthly premiums are United’s AARP Medicare Rx Preferred at $204.50, up from $126.00 in 2026, and Humana Premier Rx Plan at $137.40, up from $119.60 in 2026.

The analysis also finds that most Part D enrollees will face a deductible of several hundred dollars for drug coverage in 2027, including 85% of MA-PD enrollees, up from 80% in 2026, and virtually all PDP enrollees, 99.8%, up from 96% in 2026, based on current enrollment. The average Part D deductible in 2027 will be $643 for PDP enrollees, up from $540 in 2026,  and $489 for MA-PD enrollees, rising from $360 in 2026. The standard Part D deductible in 2027 is $700, up from $615 in 2026.

Most Part D enrollees will also face coinsurance rather than fixed copayments for preferred-brand and non-preferred drugs, potentially making their out-of-pocket costs less predictable. Virtually all PDP enrollees will face median coinsurance of 25% for preferred brands and 35% for non-preferred drugs, according to KFF.

Tatiana Bilbao creates rammed-earth installation at Art Omi

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Mexico-based architecture practice Tatiana Bilbao Estudio has created a large-scale outdoor installation made of rammed earth derived from local soil that’s meant to break down over time for New York arts centre Art Omi.

Tatiana Bilbao Estudio‘s Borrowed Earth installation consists of four 7.5-foot-tall rammed-earth cruciform crosses, with a shorter cross form in the centre that serves as a bench. Each form was made with compacted soil harvested from the Hudson Valley area.

Tatiana Bilbao Estudio based the design around a “repeatable basic unit” that eventually became the bench and surrounding columns.

The Borrowed Earth piece was commissioned by arts centre Art Omi, which oversees a 120-acre sculpture park at its campus in Ghent, New York.

Art Omi curator Julia van den Hout said rammed earth can be a “challenging material”, and it shaped much of the project’s design.

“They were not always crosses in our initial conversations,” van den Hout told Dezeen.

“As we started getting into the reality of trying to figure out how to construct this – because rammed earth is a challenging material to work with – and thinking structurally about how this would be built up, during those conversations, [it] shifted to being a cruciform column.”

“It also really resonated [because] it has such a strong classic modernist shape.”

The Art Omi and Tatiana Bilbao Estudio teams constructed the columns on site with guidance from a rammed-earth consultant based out of Mexico.

The team used soil from a construction site just down the road from Art Omi, according to van den Hout. They mixed it with sand and lime and then packed the mixture layer by layer into wooden formwork to create the sculptures.

Van den Hout says the installation is designed to decay over a period of about two years, although the team does not have “a strict answer” about how long it will last given external elements like weather.

“It’ll be an experiment,” she said. “We don’t quite know how fast it’s going to go. It is definitely soft. It crumbles. It’s lovely and tactile in that when you touch it, you really feel that sense that erosion can happen pretty easily. But simultaneously, they feel very stable.”

Recently, MOS Architects created a sculpture at Art Omi made of aluminium, while Young Projects installed a frayed, wooden pavilion at the park in 2024.

Tatiana Bilbao Estudio completed a sprawling aquarium in 2023 in Mexico, where the studio is primarily based and also spoke to Dezeen in 2019 about the country’s rising generation of prominent female architects.

Portugal : Grande première pour le fils de Cristiano Ronaldo !

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

C’est une étape majeure dans la jeune carrière de Cristiano Ronaldo Júnior. Lundi 2 novembre 2026, la Fédération portugaise de football a officialisé la présence de l'attaquant de 16 ans dans la liste des U17 pour disputer les éliminatoires de l'Euro 2027.

Le sélectionneur portugais Filipe Ramos a retenu le fils de Cristiano Ronaldo dans une liste de 23 joueurs dévoilée le lundi 2 novembre 2026 par la FPF. Évoluant au sein des catégories de jeunes d'Al-Nassr en Arabie saoudite, le joueur polyvalent, capable d'évoluer en pointe comme sur les ailes, totalise 3 buts en 7 apparitions cette saison sous les couleurs du club saoudien. Une belle marque de confiance. Le fils aîné de Cristiano Ronaldo franchit ainsi un palier très attendu en intégrant pour la première fois cette catégorie d'âge.

Cette arrivée chez les moins de 17 ans vient récompenser un parcours déjà très abouti avec les équipes de jeunes de la Seleção. En effet, Cristiano Ronaldo Jr avait fêté ses grands débuts internationaux le 13 mai 2025 avec les U15, catégorie avec laquelle il s'était illustré en remportant le tournoi Vlatko Markovic grâce à un doublé en finale. Promu chez les U16 dès le 30 octobre 2025, il y a compilé 3 buts en 9 matches et remporté deux compétitions, jusqu'à sa dernière sortie le 24 mai 2026. Au total, son bilan s'élève à 13 sélections et 5 buts avec le Portugal.

Même si son avenir en sélection senior n'est pas encore définitivement scellé au regard des règles de la FIFA, Cristiano Ronaldo Jr confirme sa fidélité au maillot portugais. L'attaquant de 16 ans disposait pourtant de plusieurs opportunités sur la scène internationale, puisqu'il était potentiellement sélectionnable par quatre autres nations. Né en Californie, il pouvait prétendre défendre les couleurs des États-Unis, mais aussi de l'Espagne en raison d'une résidence de plus de trois ans, de l'Angleterre après le passage de son père à Manchester, ou encore du Cap-Vert grâce aux origines de sa grand-mère paternelle. Toutefois, le joueur poursuit son chemin sous la tunique portugaise.

Le stage de préparation débutera dès le 6 novembre 2026 au centre fédéral de la Cidade do Futebol, à Oeiras dans la banlieue de Lisbonne. Pour accéder au tour suivant des qualifications de l'Euro U17 2027, le Portugal devra terminer parmi les deux premiers du groupe 6 lors des trois matches prévus à domicile contre l'Andorre le 11 novembre, la Bulgarie le 14 novembre et la Serbie le 17 novembre 2026. Au-delà des U17, le regard se tourne aussi vers Cristiano Ronaldo senior (41 ans). Après avoir purgé une suspension de 30 jours et 255 euros d'amende suite à un désaccord avec Jorge Jesus, son retour est attendu selon la presse portugaise dans la liste de l'équipe A qui sera annoncée le vendredi 6 novembre 2026.

Google will let schools skip AI tools, but they have to give up search, too

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Google will offer schools AI tools that purportedly don’t give away the answers.

While Google is trying to move beyond Chromebooks, the ubiquity of these devices has made the company’s education products huge in schools. Now, Google says it will let educators choose whether to allow its AI tools in the classroom. However, blocking AI tools also means giving up search, leaving schools with a difficult decision.

Google’s new overarching approach to AI in K-12 classrooms sounds good on the surface. After consulting educators, researchers, and lawmakers, Google has decided that schools using its education products will be opted out of AI tools by default. It will be up to administrators to manually enable them when these changes roll out in the coming months.

When you look at what Google considers to be an AI tool, the nature of the Faustian bargain becomes clear. The affected products are Gemini, Gemini Notebook, Gemini in Classroom, and Google Search. There’s no picking and choosing—you either allow all the AI or none of it.

This is actually consistent with Google’s approach in the consumer space. Google executives have said in no uncertain terms that Google Search is AI search. Most searches now provide an AI Overview at the top, and the company is constantly coming up with new ways to nudge people toward the conversational AI Mode chatbot function. Google sees this as the future.

In a statement to The New York Times, Google education VP Chris Phillips reiterated this approach to web search. “Search and AI are deeply integrated,” he said, adding that if a school chooses not to enable AI, the students “will not have access to search, and they’ll see a message from their school that indicates it’s not available.”

Google could make a version of search for schools that doesn’t have AI tools, but it’s not doing that. Instead, it’s creating two different versions of AI in search. When schools enable AI for student accounts, they’ll have two options: the standard Gemini-based AI and the new “Guided Learning” version. The latter, available in Classroom as well as search, is supposed to provide one-on-one tutoring and assistance without giving away all the answers. Google notes all of its generative AI products can make “mistakes,” though, so it may not be difficult to talk the chatbot into doing your homework.

Google is betting that schools won’t want to give up Google Search and will therefore accept AI tools. This will expose students to AI early and often, possibly changing the way they interact with technology. Schools that don’t like Google’s approach could switch to alternatives like DuckDuckGo, which lets you disable AI features. But will Google’s new Guided Learning be enough to assuage their concerns?

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

The Best Hiking Child Carriers for Travel, Day Hikes, and Long Trails

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

If you buy through our links, we may earn an affiliate commission. This supports our mission to get more people active and outside.Learn about Outside Online's affiliate link policy

Before our daughter AJ arrived, my wife and I braced for the end of an era. We figured our adventurous lifestyle would be long gone (or at least forever changed); our family trips would revolve around nap schedules instead of trails; and our days of spontaneity and exploration would grind to a halt. Turns out, we couldn’t have been more wrong.

At eighteen months, our daughter has probably logged more trail miles than the average adult. She’s joined us on ridgeline hikes in the Blue Ridge Mountains, alpine lake loops in the Rockies, coastal walks in New Brunswick, red-rock rambles in Arches and Zion national parks, and plenty of urban outings around Chicago.

And all the credit goes to the unsung hero of our gear closet: the hiking child carrier.

While she loves to explore the trail on foot in short stints under a mile (for now), we’ve found that she’s most content at longer distances taking in the view from above our shoulders. And when we’re lucky, she’ll even doze off for an afternoon nap mid-hike. We’ve tested several kid carriers on dozens of hikes, looking for the ones that balance comfort, fit, and storage. These are the ones we reach for again and again on our hiking adventures.

We take a lot of hiking-centric trips, and packing for them with a toddler is already a puzzle. Between a Pack ’n Play, a car seat, and a week’s worth of diapers, there’s not much bandwidth to lug along a bulky carrier. The Osprey Poco LT is the exception. At five pounds, nine ounces, and a whopping twenty-five liters of gear storage (nearly double what you’ll find in the Kelty and Deuter carriers below), its weight-to-capacity ratio stomps the competition.

Its internal aluminum frame keeps the pack light but provides enough structure and strength to carry a toddler up to forty pounds. There’s a massive lower zipper compartment that fits extra layers or a change of clothes, a soft cooler with snacks, and diapers with room to spare. And a built-in UPF 50 sunshade unfolds and clips to keep the sun off our kid’s sensitive skin.

But the real magic is in how it packs down. The Poco LT folds completely flat and can easily fit into an airplane’s overhead bin or other tight spaces like a jam-packed trunk on a road trip. I can’t say that about any other child carrier I’ve tested. Since getting this pack, we’ve completely ditched the stroller at the airport in favor of this pack as a lightweight transport option for our kiddo.

The tradeoffs? Thinner shoulder straps and a minimalist design mean it’s not the pack for very long days on the trail. Its shorter hip belt and slim profile also don’t fit larger bodies as well as some of the alternatives below. But for traveling to a place you expect to hike or walk five miles or less, it’s the ideal pick.

For smaller babies not big enough for a full-blown backpack carrier, Osprey also makes the Poco Soft Child Carrier. It cradles your young’un on the front of your body, and once they’re big enough, they can face outward to enjoy the view.

Gearing up for a day hike (especially with a kid) means bringing plenty of extras along for the ride: snacks, layers, sunscreen, bug spray, more snacks, and diapers—to name a few. Two pounds heavier than the Poco LT, this pack certainly feels bulkier. But what it lacks in packability it makes up for in convenient features. If you care less about cutting weight and more about pockets, comfort for your kiddo, and space, the Kelty Journey PerfectFIT Elite is your best bet.

Nine total pockets—including three front zippered pockets in different sizes, dual zippered hip pockets, and large mesh side pockets—offer ample storage for a day hike. We’re big fans of the zippered “dirty” compartment on the underside of the pack to stash soiled clothes, like when an unexpected diaper blowout strikes mid-hike. Six chunky grab handles make it easy to hoist the carrier (with some help from a partner or friend) onto your back or set it down gently. Once it’s down, an oversized kickstand keeps the pack sturdy and balanced, even when your kiddo’s kicking around inside. Kelty clearly cut no corners on the details with this pack.

A pack suitable for sleep is non-negotiable. Who wouldn’t want the serenity of their kid snoozing mid-hike? More miles, fewer meltdowns, as I like to say. AJ zonks out in the Journey PerfectFIT Elite more than in any other carrier. I suspect it’s the roomy seat and oversized chin pad (perfect for resting her head like a pillow), which seem to make settling in for a nap that much easier. We took her on numerous day hikes in the Blue Ridge Mountains around Asheville this fall, and she fell asleep on nearly all of them when we timed our outings around her usual naptime. Now, I’m not promising this pack will put your kid to sleep. But I’m confident its cozy, supportive setup will make a mid-hike nap that much more likely.

Backpacking families and peak-bagging parents who want to take on bigger mileage need a carrier that balances weight, capacity, and comfort. For longer hikes, I grab the Deuter Kid Comfort because it fits like a classic backpacking pack. Think of it as an overnight pack that happens to carry a kid instead of your tent, sleeping pad, and stove.

Fit is where the Kid Comfort shines. A sliding torso adjustment lets whoever’s wearing it (Mama, Momzie, Grandma, or Uncle Daniel, in AJ’s case) dial in a precise fit. And as a notoriously sweaty hiker, I appreciated that the back panel’s raised and ventilated mesh kept air moving without trapping heat on my back. The Kid Comfort’s height-adjustable seat has a cushion that widens as your kid grows. We started AJ in it at ten months old, and at eighteen months, she still rides comfortably with plenty of room for continued use over the next couple of years.

It’s eight ounces heavier than the Journey PerfectFIT Elite, but its greater adjustability and plusher padding make it that much more comfortable on longer, more technical routes. On a recent trip to Arches and Zion national parks, we spent long days exploring the trails. I noticed far less shoulder and hip fatigue with the Kid Comfort compared to the Osprey Poco LT and Kelty Journey PerfectFIT Elite packs, especially given all the scrambling over obstacles or on longer hikes. Ergonomic hip wings secure the weight of the load above your hips. Because the hip belt locks the load in place and the pack doesn’t shift or sway with every step, I felt confident scrambling up and down sandstone, squeezing through slot canyons, and crossing exposed slickrock with AJ on my back.

The tradeoff is its limited storage. At fourteen liters, it holds roughly half as much as the Kelty and Osprey packs. Two stretchy side pockets, hip belt pockets, a large stretch pocket on the back, and a zippered bottom compartment hold the essentials but not much more. It’s enough for a day-long outing, but one of us always also ends up carrying a daypack in addition to the Deuter so we can have everything we need. All to say: This pack is by far the most secure and comfortable on technical trails, especially over longer distances.

Floating Alone Down the Brahmaputra on a Bamboo Raft

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Indian mountaineer and adventurer Shekhar Bordoloi has just started a solo expedition down the Brahmaputra River. He set off on a homemade on a bamboo raft from the Dhola–Sadiya Bridge on November 1 and aims to finish at Dhubri, 891km later. The route covers the entire Brahmaputra within India and should take 22 to 25 days in all. The river begins in Tibet and continues beyond India into Bangladesh, covering 2,900km in all.

This won’t be Bordoloi’s first time down the river. In 2020, he and two friends took a similar bamboo raft down a 400km section of the Brahmaputra. Earlier, in 2015, Bordoloi was one of 11 who tackled another part of the river over 11 days under the auspices of the Assam Mountaineering Association. This expedition, however, will test his endurance, pitting him against river conditions for longer, and with less support, than ever before.

Bordoloi on a previous raft expedition on the Brahmaputra. This raft took Bordoloi and friends from the world’s largest river island, Umananda, to its smallest, Majuli. Photo: Shekhar Bordoloi/Instagram

Bordoloi’s raft was hand-built from bamboo poles carefully lashed together over several days. Such craft are a traditional way of navigating rivers across the region, and are still used by local communities for fishing and transportation.

Though the river can be temperamental, Bordoloi’s first two days have been relatively calm, taking him down the Lohit, one of the Brahmaputra’s many tributaries and channels. Navigating the complex river system alone will be a challenge. The Brahmaputra is one of the largest river systems in India, the ninth-largest in the world by discharge, and is prone to flooding and powerful currents.

But according to Bordoloi, those obscure, winding corridors are at the heart of this project. Many sections of the river remain underexplored, and he believes there is hidden potential for the people in his home region of Assam. If he can map routes for river tourism and model environmentally responsible river navigation through those routes, he hopes to draw attention to this important riverine ecosystem and the economic opportunities for the people living along it.

The logistical challenges of a long expedition will further complicate progress. On previous expeditions, Bordoloi traveled with minimal supplies or no food at all, relying entirely on foraging and knowledge of the natural landscape. But this adventure will take over twice as long and may put those skills to their limits.

Bordoloi is documenting his daily progress on Facebook and Instagram.

Lou Bodenhemier holds an MA in History from the University of Limerick and a BA in Creative Writing from the University of Arizona. He’s interested in maritime and disaster history as well as criminal history, and his dissertation focused on the werewolf trials of early modern Europe. At the present moment he can most likely be found perusing records of shipboard crime and punishment during the Age of Sail, or failing that, writing historical fiction horror stories. He lives in Dublin and hates the sun.
