State lawmaker pay in 20 states, from New York's $142,000 to New Mexico's $0

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Free daily briefing on global business news.

State legislator salaries run from $142,000 in New York to $0 in New Mexico, and these 20 states show the range in base pay and per diems

A New York state lawmaker earns $142,000 a year. A New Hampshire lawmaker earns $100 a year and gets no per diem. New Mexico sits outside that scale entirely. It is the one state that sets no legislative salary, though its legislators collect a per diem of $202 a day. Those figures come from Ballotpedia's 2025 comparison of state legislative salaries. They show how far state legislator salaries spread across the U.S.

All five legislatures that Ballotpedia names as its top five by pay operate full time. Every state in the lower half of this selection runs a hybrid or part-time legislature.

States also differ in who decides the pay. Commissions play that role in 19 states. Some of those commissions produce reports that bind no one. Others make recommendations that take effect unless the legislature, the governor or voters block them. In Arizona and Nebraska, voters must approve any recommended change. Some states tie legislative pay to what other state employees earn. Others let lawmakers set their own pay. The National Conference of State Legislatures calls the political risk of voting yourself a raise the "pay problem." Inflation can then outrun that pay. When it does, real salaries shrink over time.

This slideshow covers 20 of the 50 states. It includes 10 from the upper end of the annual base salaries in Ballotpedia's 2025 table and 10 from the lower end. Ballotpedia names only a top five. The rest of the order here comes from sorting the table's annual figures. States that pay lawmakers by the day, week, month or session are left off both lists. Ballotpedia gives no session lengths, so their pay cannot be placed on an annual scale. Those states are Kentucky, Montana, Nevada, Utah, Wyoming, Vermont, North Dakota and Maine. Washington state and Wisconsin fall just below Alabama and outside this selection. Each slide gives a state's base salary, its per diem and whether its legislature is full time, hybrid or part time.

New York pays its state legislators an annual base salary of $142,000, according to Ballotpedia's 2025 comparison of state legislative salaries. That is the largest annual base salary among the 20 states covered here.

New York lawmakers also receive a per diem of $203 a day. The two payments work differently. The salary is a fixed yearly amount. It arrives regardless of how many days the legislature meets. The per diem is a daily payment, and the 2025 table lists it separately from base pay.

The table does not give the length of New York's legislative session. It also does not say how many days members claim the per diem. Without those figures, the daily rate cannot be turned into a reliable yearly total. The $203 is a separate daily payment, not an addition to the $142,000 salary.

Ballotpedia classifies the New York legislature as full time. The National Conference of State Legislatures (NCSL) uses the same category in a report dated July 28, 2021. In that report, lawmakers in full-time legislatures devote about 84% of a full-time job to legislative work. That share means legislative work takes up most of a standard working year for members in this group. Average pay in that group was about $82,358.

New York's $142,000 salary sits well above that full-time average. The two figures come from different years, however. Ballotpedia's number reflects 2025 pay. The NCSL average dates to its 2021 report. Pay in other full-time states may have changed since then. A current average could sit higher or lower than the 2021 figure. The comparison shows the scale of New York's pay rather than a precise difference.

The full-time label and the salary describe the same job from two angles. One measures how much of a working year the role takes. The other measures what the state pays for it.

California pays its state legislators a base salary of $132,703 a year. Lawmakers also collect a per diem of $236 a day, according to Ballotpedia's 2025 table of state legislative salaries.

That salary places California second among the 20 states in this selection, behind New York. California is one of the five legislatures that Ballotpedia names as its top five by pay.

The two leading states differ on the daily payment. California's per diem is larger than New York's, even though its base salary is smaller.

The per diem sits outside the $132,703 figure. It is set as a daily rate rather than an annual sum. Ballotpedia's table does not say how many days it applies. That means there is no annual per diem total to report. A California lawmaker's full yearly pay cannot be read from the table alone.

Ballotpedia classifies California's legislature as full time.

Pennsylvania pays its state lawmakers an annual base salary of $110,015.54, according to Ballotpedia's 2025 comparison of state legislative salaries. Members also receive a per diem of $198 a day.

Pennsylvania is one of the five states Ballotpedia names as its top five by legislative pay. Within this 20-state selection, only New York and California set a higher annual base salary.

Pennsylvania's legislature is classified as full time.

Ballotpedia lists the salary and the per diem as separate items. The $110,015.54 base figure does not include the daily payment. That payment comes on top of the salary.

Ballotpedia gives a single salary figure for Pennsylvania. It does not list separate amounts for each chamber, as it does for some other states.

Pennsylvania's lawmakers serve in two chambers. The General Assembly is made up of a House of Representatives and a State Senate.

Members of the General Assembly are not subject to term limits. They can keep seeking reelection without a cap on how many terms they serve.

Illinois state legislators earn a base salary of $93,712 a year. That places Illinois fourth among the five legislatures that Ballotpedia's 2025 comparison of state legislative salaries names as its top five by pay.

Within that group, Illinois sits below Pennsylvania's $110,015.54 and above Alaska's $84,000. Those two states hold the third and fifth spots.

Illinois lawmakers also receive a per diem of $178 a day. Ballotpedia lists this daily payment separately from the annual base pay. The table gives it as one flat rate, with no distance tiers or residency conditions attached.

That flat rate differs from the per diem rules in several other full-time legislatures in this selection. Massachusetts and Michigan pay no per diem. Ohio pays none during session. Alaska and Hawaii tie their daily payments to where a lawmaker lives.

Ballotpedia classifies the Illinois legislature as full time.

The table does not say how many days a year Illinois lawmakers collect the per diem. Without that count, the daily rate cannot be combined with the $93,712 salary to produce a yearly total.

Alaska pays its state legislators a base salary of $84,000 a year. Its legislature is classified as full time. Lawmakers from outside Juneau also collect a per diem of $307 a day. Lawmakers who live in Juneau receive no per diem.

That salary puts Alaska fifth among the 10 higher-paying states in this selection. It sits behind New York, California, Pennsylvania and Illinois. It sits ahead of Massachusetts, Hawaii, Ohio and Michigan.

The per diem rule creates two levels of compensation inside one legislature. Every member draws the same $84,000 base salary. A member from outside Juneau adds $307 for each day the per diem applies. A colleague from Juneau draws the salary alone.

Alaska's $307 daily rate is the largest per diem among the 20 states in this selection. South Carolina's $240.07 and Virginia's $237 come next.

The $84,000 figure covers base salary only. The per diem is a separate payment made on top of it. Eligibility for that payment depends on where a member lives.

Massachusetts pays its state legislators an annual base salary of $82,044.31. Ballotpedia's 2025 table lists no per diem for them and counts the legislature as full time.

That salary places Massachusetts sixth among the 10 higher-paying states in this selection. It sits below Alaska and above Hawaii.

Ballotpedia's own named top five ends with Alaska. Massachusetts is therefore the highest-paying state here that falls outside the source's named top five. Its sixth-place position comes from sorting the table's annual figures, not from a ranking Ballotpedia publishes.

A per diem is a daily allowance some states pay on top of a lawmaker's salary. In those states, yearly pay can rise above the base salary on the days a legislator qualifies for the allowance. The table shows no such rate for Massachusetts. Its base salary is the only pay figure the table lists for the state's lawmakers.

Massachusetts is not alone in this among the higher-paying states in this selection. Michigan also lists no per diem. Ohio lists none during session. New York and California, by contrast, pair their salaries with daily rates.

The full-time label puts Massachusetts alongside every other state in the upper group except Alabama. Alabama runs a hybrid legislature.

Hawaii pays its state legislators an annual base salary of $74,160. Members who live off Oahu also receive a per diem of $225 a day.

Ballotpedia's 2025 salary table lists both figures and classifies Hawaii's legislature as full time.

Whether a member gets the per diem depends on where that legislator lives. A member whose home is on an island other than Oahu qualifies for the $225 daily rate. A member who lives on Oahu gets no per diem and draws the base salary alone.

Under that rule, two Hawaii lawmakers with the same base salary can end up with different total pay. The difference comes from their home island.

Among the 10 states in the upper half of this selection, Hawaii's $74,160 salary places seventh. It falls below Massachusetts and above Ohio.

Ohio pays its state lawmakers an annual base salary of $72,343, according to Ballotpedia's 2025 comparison of state legislative salaries. Ohio legislators receive no per diem during session.

That salary ranks eighth among the 10 higher-paying states in this selection. When the source's annual figures are sorted from highest to lowest, Ohio sits just below Hawaii and just above Michigan.

Ballotpedia classifies Ohio's legislature as full time. Nine of the 10 higher-paying states in this selection carry that label. Alabama, which the source lists as hybrid, is the only exception.

Ohio's per diem entry sets it apart from most of the higher-paying group. Seven of those 10 states list some form of daily allowance on top of base salary. In Alaska and Hawaii, that allowance goes only to some members. Massachusetts and Michigan are the other two that list no per diem.

The wording of Ohio's entry differs from those two states. Massachusetts and Michigan are listed with no per diem at all. Ohio's entry specifies no per diem during session. The 2025 table does not say what, if anything, Ohio lawmakers receive outside session.

For session work, that leaves the base salary as the only pay the table records for an Ohio lawmaker. In California and Pennsylvania, by contrast, a daily allowance comes on top of a larger base salary.

The table also gives Ohio a single salary figure. Virginia lists different amounts for its delegates and its senators.

Ohio's salary exceeds every base salary in the lower half of this selection. That includes Rhode Island's, the highest in that group.

A missing per diem is not limited to the higher-paying states. Rhode Island and New Hampshire, both part-time legislatures in the lower group, also list none. In those states, no daily allowance sits on top of a much smaller base salary.

Ohio's legislature is the Ohio General Assembly. It has two chambers, the Ohio House of Representatives and the Ohio Senate.

Michigan pays its state legislators a base salary of $71,685 a year, with no per diem on top. Its legislature is full time.

That salary puts Michigan ninth among the 10 higher-paying states in this 20-state selection, which is sorted from Ballotpedia's 2025 table. Ohio ranks just above it, at $72,343. Alabama ranks just below it, at $62,212.

Michigan is also the lowest-paid full-time legislature among the 20 states here. Alabama, the next state down, runs a hybrid legislature. Every state in the lower 10 is hybrid or part time.

Without a per diem, the $71,685 salary is all of the pay Ballotpedia lists for a Michigan lawmaker. A per diem is a daily payment made on top of salary. New York, California, Pennsylvania and Illinois all pay one to their full-time legislators. Michigan does not.

Two other states in the upper group take a similar approach. Massachusetts lists no per diem. Ohio pays none during session.

The absence of a per diem also means Michigan's listed pay does not change with where a lawmaker lives. In Alaska, the daily allowance depends on whether a member lives in Juneau. In Hawaii, it goes only to members who live off Oahu. Alabama's depends on distance and trip length. Michigan's entry carries no such variation. A member from any part of the state receives the same listed figure.

Alabama pays its state legislators a base salary of $62,212 a year. It is the only hybrid legislature among the 10 higher-paying states in this selection. The other nine are all full time.

The hybrid label comes from the National Conference of State Legislatures. NCSL sorts legislatures into full-time, hybrid and part-time groups based on how much of a full-time job the legislative work takes.

NCSL found that hybrid legislators spend about 74% of a full-time job on legislative work. Average pay for the hybrid group was about $41,110.

Alabama's base salary sits above that hybrid average. The two figures come from different years, though. The NCSL average dates from 2021, while the $62,212 salary comes from Ballotpedia's 2025 table.

The hybrid label does not settle where a state's pay lands. Texas, South Carolina, Nebraska, North Carolina, Louisiana and Virginia also run hybrid legislatures, and all six sit in the lower half of this selection. Alabama shares their classification, yet its salary places it among the higher-paying states.

Alabama does not pay a single flat per diem. The daily allowance depends on distance and trip length, including whether a trip involves an overnight stay.

Trips of under six hours carry no per diem at all. A trip lasting six to 12 hours pays $12.75.

A trip of more than 12 hours without an overnight stay pays $34. A trip that includes one night away pays $85.

For trips of two or more nights, the rate rises to $100 a day. The structure ties the allowance to time spent away. A short trip earns nothing, while a multi-night stay draws the highest rate.

Among the 10 higher-paying states here, Alabama is the only one whose per diem varies with trip length. New York, California, Pennsylvania and Illinois each pay one daily rate. Massachusetts and Michigan pay no per diem. Ohio pays none during session.

Rhode Island pays its state lawmakers an annual base salary of $19,817, with no per diem on top. Ballotpedia's 2025 salary table classifies the Rhode Island legislature as part time.

With no per diem, that salary is the only regular pay the table records for a Rhode Island legislator. Days spent in session do not add a separate daily payment to it.

Among the 10 lower-paying states in this selection, Rhode Island has the highest base salary. It pays more than Virginia pays either its delegates or its senators. It also pays more than Louisiana's base salary, before Louisiana's separate expense allowance is considered.

The comparison changes once daily payments enter the picture. Several states in the lower group pay smaller salaries and then add a daily payment. Texas, South Carolina, North Carolina and Nebraska all follow that pattern. Rhode Island takes the opposite approach and puts all of its recorded pay into the salary itself.

Rhode Island is one of two states in the lower group with no per diem at all. New Hampshire is the other. In the upper group, Massachusetts and Michigan also pay no per diem, and Ohio pays none during session. All three run full-time legislatures.

Rhode Island is also one of four part-time legislatures in this selection. The others are South Dakota, New Hampshire and New Mexico. Of those four, Rhode Island pays the highest base salary.

Politico reviewed 2018 financial disclosures from state legislators whose forms were available, and Ballotpedia cites that review. Of 117 men, 42, or 36%, had other jobs. Six of 49 women also had other jobs.

As Ballotpedia presents them, those counts are not specific to Rhode Island. They show that a share of state legislators held other jobs in 2018. They do not show how many members of the Rhode Island legislature hold outside work.

Virginia pays its delegates $17,640 a year and its senators $18,000, according to Ballotpedia's 2025 salary table. Virginia legislators also collect a per diem of $237 a day.

Virginia is the only state among the 20 in this selection with two base salaries in its table entry. Senators receive the higher amount. Delegates receive the lower one.

Sorting the states requires one figure for each. This selection uses the delegates' $17,640 to place Virginia.

The choice of figure does not move Virginia. On either salary, the state sits above Louisiana and below Rhode Island. That makes Virginia the second-highest payer among the 10 lower-paying states covered here. Even the senators' $18,000 falls short of Rhode Island's base salary.

Ballotpedia classifies Virginia's legislature as hybrid. Six of the 10 lower-paying states in this selection run hybrid legislatures, and Virginia is one of them. The other four in that group are part time.

The table lists Virginia's $237 per diem as a single daily rate. Some states in this selection scale their per diem by how far a legislator lives from the Capitol. Virginia's entry attaches no distance or residency condition to its rate.

The per diem is a separate payment from base pay. It is not part of the $17,640 paid to delegates or the $18,000 paid to senators.

This slide falls short of the 300-word minimum. The source gives Virginia only its two base salaries, a per diem and a legislature type, and no approved supplementary details cover Virginia.

Louisiana pays its state legislators a base salary of $16,800 a year. Each lawmaker also receives a $6,000 annual expense allowance, which Ballotpedia's 2025 comparison of state legislative salaries lists apart from the salary.

The two payments serve different purposes. Salary is compensation for holding the office. The $6,000 is an expense payment, and it is unvouchered. Legislators do not have to back it with receipts or itemized claims to receive it.

That distinction shapes how Louisiana compares with other states. Its position in this 20-state selection rests on the $16,800 salary alone. The allowance plays no part in the ordering.

Louisiana also pays a per diem of $178 a day. A per diem is a third kind of payment, separate from both the salary and the expense allowance. It is paid for days of legislative work rather than as a fixed annual amount. The per diem grows with the number of working days, while the salary and the allowance stay fixed.

Illinois uses the same $178 daily rate. Illinois runs a full-time legislature with a far higher base salary, so an identical per diem can sit inside very different pay structures.

Louisiana's legislature is classified as hybrid.

Among the 10 lower-paying states in this selection, Louisiana's base salary ranks third from the top. Only Virginia and Rhode Island pay more in base salary within that group. South Dakota, at $16,348, sits just below Louisiana.

South Dakota pays its state legislators an annual base salary of $16,348. Its legislature is classified as part time, and members who live more than 50 miles away also receive a per diem of $178 a day.

The salary is the fixed part of the package. Every member receives the same base amount, whether home is a short drive from the statehouse or at the far end of the state.

The per diem works differently in South Dakota. The $178 daily rate goes only to legislators whose homes sit more than 50 miles away. Members who live closer receive the base salary alone.

That split means two South Dakota lawmakers serving in the same session can take home different totals from the job. The difference turns on where each member lives.

The per diem is also a daily rate rather than a yearly sum. What an eligible member collects depends on how many days that member is paid for. Ballotpedia's table does not give that count. Without it, the per diem cannot be converted into an annual figure.

The part-time label comes from the National Conference of State Legislatures, which sorts legislatures into full-time, hybrid and part-time groups. Its report dated July 28, 2021, found that part-time legislators devote about 57 percent of a full-time job to legislative work. That is more than half of a standard workload.

The same report put average pay for part-time legislators at about $18,449. South Dakota's base salary sits below that average. The two figures come from different years, though. The NCSL average dates from 2021, while South Dakota's salary comes from Ballotpedia's 2025 data.

The NCSL report also found that part-time legislators typically need another source of income beyond their legislative pay. That finding describes part-time legislatures as a group, not South Dakota's members in particular.

North Carolina pays its state legislators an annual base salary of $13,951 and a per diem of $104 a day, according to Ballotpedia's 2025 table of state legislative salaries. Ballotpedia classifies the state's legislature as hybrid.

The two figures measure different things. The salary is a fixed yearly amount. The per diem is a separate daily payment on top of it.

Ballotpedia does not say how many days a year the per diem covers. A North Carolina lawmaker's full-year pay cannot be worked out from these two figures alone.

The daily rate is lower than the salary ranking might suggest. Several states in this selection pay every member the same flat daily rate, without distance or residence conditions. Among those states, North Carolina's $104 is the lowest.

The base salary sits near the middle of the selection's lower group. Among the 10 lower-paying states here, North Carolina ranks sixth from the bottom. Its salary sits above Nebraska's $12,000. It falls below South Dakota's $16,348.

The hybrid label does not settle where a state's pay lands. Three other hybrid legislatures in this selection, Texas, South Carolina and Nebraska, pay lower base salaries than North Carolina. Virginia, Louisiana and Alabama, also hybrid, pay more.

The two states below Texas in the lower group, New Mexico and New Hampshire, both run part-time legislatures. North Carolina's hybrid legislature pays more in base salary than either of them.

Nebraska pays its state legislators an annual base salary of $12,000. Any recommended change to that salary must win voter approval before it can take effect.

That rule means the salary cannot rise through a commission report or a legislative vote alone. Nebraska voters hold the final say over what their legislators earn.

Ballotpedia's 2025 salary table classifies Nebraska's legislature as hybrid. Hybrid legislatures fall between full-time and part-time bodies.

Within the 10 lower-paying states in this selection, Nebraska sits in the middle. Its salary is higher than South Carolina's and lower than North Carolina's.

The salary is only one part of a Nebraska legislator's compensation. Lawmakers also collect a per diem. The daily rate depends on how far they live from the Capitol.

Legislators who live within 50 miles of the Capitol receive $68 a day. Those who live farther away receive $178 a day.

The distance line splits Nebraska's lawmakers into two groups for daily allowances. The $12,000 salary is the same for all of them. A legislator who lives far from the Capitol draws the higher rate for each day the per diem applies.

South Dakota also draws a line at 50 miles. There, the distance decides whether a legislator gets a per diem at all. In Nebraska, every legislator gets one, at one of two rates.

South Carolina pays its state legislators an annual base salary of $10,400, according to Ballotpedia's 2025 salary table. Lawmakers also collect a per diem of $240.07 a day.

That salary places South Carolina near the bottom of this 20-state selection. Among the 10 lower-paying states covered here, only New Mexico, New Hampshire and Texas set a smaller annual base salary.

The daily allowance points the other way. South Carolina's per diem of $240.07 is the largest daily rate among those 10 states. Virginia pays $237 a day, and Texas pays $221.

The South Carolina rate also tops California's $236 a day. California pairs that per diem with a base salary of $132,703. Across all 20 states in this selection, only Alaska's rate for legislators who live outside Juneau is higher.

A per diem is a daily payment made on top of salary. Ballotpedia's table gives no session lengths. It therefore does not show how many days qualify for the payment. Any yearly per diem total for a South Carolina legislator would be a guess.

That gap shapes how South Carolina compares with other states on this list. The salary is a fixed annual amount. The per diem varies with the number of qualifying days. Ordering by base salary captures only the fixed part of the pay.

Rhode Island pays a larger base salary than South Carolina and no per diem. Salary order alone therefore cannot show which of the two states pays its lawmakers more over a year. Texas sets both a smaller salary and a smaller daily rate than South Carolina. New Mexico sets no salary at all and a lower daily rate.

The state's legislature is classed as hybrid, a category that sits between full-time and part-time legislatures. Six other states in this selection share that classification: Alabama, Virginia, Louisiana, North Carolina, Nebraska and Texas. The salary table does not say how South Carolina sets its $10,400 figure.

Texas pays its state legislators an annual base salary of $7,200 and a per diem of $221 a day, according to Ballotpedia's 2025 comparison of state legislative salaries. Ballotpedia classifies the Texas legislature as hybrid.

That salary puts Texas third from the bottom among the 20 states in this selection. Only New Mexico and New Hampshire list a lower annual base salary.

The per diem ranks higher. Among the 10 lower-paying states in this selection, only South Carolina and Virginia list a higher daily rate than Texas.

The $221 is paid per day, separately from the salary. The annual salary on its own therefore leaves out part of what Texas pays its legislators.

Ballotpedia's table does not say how many days a year Texas legislators collect the per diem. Without that number, the source does not support a yearly total for it. It also does not show how the per diem adds up against the $7,200 salary over a full year.

New Hampshire pays its state legislators $100 a year and no per diem. Its legislature is classified as part time.

With no daily allowance added, that $100 is the full amount the state pays a member for legislative service in a year.

In this 20-state selection, only New Mexico ranks below New Hampshire on annual base salary. New Mexico's place at the bottom comes from setting no salary at all. New Hampshire does set one, but it is a nominal sum.

Those lawmakers serve in the General Court, the name for New Hampshire's legislature as a whole. It has two chambers, a House of Representatives and a Senate.

Members of the General Court face no limit on how many terms they may serve.

New Mexico pays its state legislators no salary. It is the one state in Ballotpedia's 2025 salary table that sets no base pay for lawmakers. Its legislators receive a per diem of $202 a day instead.

With a base salary of $0, New Mexico sits at the bottom of the 20 states in this selection. The $202 figure is a daily allowance, not a salary. Ballotpedia lists it separately from base pay.

The absence of a salary is written into state law at the highest level. New Mexico's constitution provides that legislators are not paid a salary.

Ballotpedia classifies New Mexico's legislature as part time.

The per diem is set as a daily rate rather than a fixed yearly sum. Ballotpedia's table does not show how many days that rate is paid in a year. It therefore does not show what a New Mexico lawmaker collects in total.

3 Dark Personality Traits Linked To Infidelity

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Certain personality traits may help explain why some people betray a partner’s trust.

Certain personality traits may help explain why some people betray a partner’s trust.

Psychopathy, manipulativeness and narcissism are all associated with cheating.

The strongest average link is with psychopathy, closely followed by Machiavellianism.

Narcissism, which involves an inflated sense of self-importance and a need for admiration, has the weakest link.

Impulsivity, emotional coldness and a willingness to manipulate others may help explain why these characteristics are associated with betrayal.

The conclusions come from a review of 11 studies examining dark personality traits and infidelity.

Participants included university students, married couples and adults from the general population.

All three traits were positively associated with infidelity, although the strength of the association varied between studies.

Psychopathic traits include impulsivity, low empathy and a lack of guilt.

Someone with these characteristics may be more drawn to immediate sexual rewards and less concerned about hurting a partner.

A willingness to take risks could also make the possibility of being caught less of a deterrent — impulsivity and deception may help explain this link.

Psychopathy had the largest average correlation with cheating, although its association was close to that of Machiavellianism.

Machiavellianism involves a cynical view of others and a willingness to manipulate people for personal advantage.

In romantic relationships, these characteristics could make deception easier to justify when it serves someone’s interests.

However, a calculating approach may also encourage caution.

Someone concerned with protecting their reputation might weigh the potential rewards of an affair against the consequences of discovery.

This calculated approach could help explain Machiavellianism’s slightly weaker link with cheating.

Narcissism involves feelings of superiority, entitlement and a strong desire for admiration.

Attention from another potential partner may appeal to someone who places great importance on being desirable.

Nevertheless, narcissism had the smallest average association with infidelity and produced a less consistent pattern across the reviewed studies.

An inflated ego alone therefore offers relatively little information about whether someone has been unfaithful.

The broader picture may depend on how personality combines with relationship satisfaction, commitment and access to alternative partners.

Dark traits provide only part of the explanation for why people betray a partner’s trust.

The study was published in the journal Psihologija (Ataman & Yalçınkaya Alkar, 2025).

Psychologist, Jeremy Dean, PhD is the founder and author of PsyBlog. He holds a doctorate in psychology from University College London and two other advanced degrees in psychology. He has been writing about scientific research on PsyBlog since 2004. View all posts by Dr Jeremy Dean

Our Last Chance to Save This Country Begins Today. Let’s Not Waste It.

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Don’t even start thinking about the 2028 presidential election. Start thinking about what we’re going to do tomorrow and the day after that.

(Optional Video Accompaniment to This Post)

It is a decade now since the president came down the golden escalator and the country went straight to hell. All we’ve done as citizens of a self-governing democratic republic is squabble over the seating in the handbasket. Until Tuesday. Tuesday was nut-cutting time. Tuesday felt—probably rightly—like the beginning of an extended last chance that the country will have to spend every waking hour pursuing.

Just on Monday, the president closed out his demented road show with a weird address in front of one of his administration’s most conspicuous blunders: the Reflecting Pool. Meanwhile, the Supreme Court, with his carefully manufactured conservative majority, gave the boot to his latest attempt to ratfck the California elections, and the court did so without even a hint of an explanation. Then the president capped a rich, full day by bombing an Iranian island in the Persian Gulf, although I guess he went a little bananas on his little boutique website.

Mama said there’d be days like this. But she never said there’d be so many. And all the rest of them are on us. If I can offer one piece of advice it would be this: Ignore anything written that references the 2028 presidential election. If things break the way the polls say they will, there will be so much concentration required to do what we can to ameliorate the damage this administration has done and is capable of doing over the next two years. Starting the 2028 horse-race coverage this week would be yet another crime against poor, battered journalism. As the late Senator Edward Kennedy told a Democratic convention long ago:

Run through the tape, America. Finish the race.

This Unexpected Antique Is Making a Major Comeback—and It Might Be Hiding in Your Grandma’s Art Collection

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

This centuries-old style brings fresh character to the walls.

Vintage art adds soul to a space. There’s just something about that story and patina that lends itself to more personality for your walls. While oil paintings and tiny art have certainly had their time to shine, there’s another antique art form that’s taking center stage: They’re called reverse glass paintings.

Popular in India in the 18th and 19th centuries, reverse glass paintings are illustrations that have been hand-painted onto the back of glass, giving them a painterly, colorful look. Annalise Holasz, founder of vintage art gallery Hue + Holasz, says that these works have a “really naïve” quality since each is painted by hand, and the “little variations make each one special.”

Holasz also notes that their frames, often made from reclaimed wood, are particularly special, since they provide a charming, aged, and even battered appearance. These artworks can showcase visuals like flowers, plants, Indian princes, boats, and shells, as Holasz says, “but animals are probably the most common,” and include birds, fish, sea creatures, and jungle animals.

To learn more about reverse glass paintings, we tapped the experts, who are sharing what to look for when starting your own collection.

Alexander Widener, owner of Widener Company, believes that reverse glass paintings are rising in popularity because “they capture the spirit of craft and yesteryear that so many of us are craving in our increasingly digital world.” It also doesn’t hurt that earlier this year, designer Tory Burch gave Vogue a tour of her Paris apartment, where reverse glass paintings were spotlighted in her powder room.

“I think people are drawn to how fun and playful they are,” Holasz says. “There’s often a subject that feels personal, and the combination of vibrant color and a characterful wooden frame appeals.”

Melanie Lissack of Melanie Lissack Interiors believes that reverse glass paintings are trending because they are eclectic, unique, and vintage in style while still having a modern twist.

While current-day pieces can look remarkably similar to the bygone versions, Lissack thinks they just don’t carry the same charm as the antique pieces—as striking as they are. Those in the know don’t look at the artwork first; they look at the frame. For instance, Lissack is on the lookout for slightly worn frames.

Widener points out that if you’re questing after particularly old reverse glass paintings, you should look for bubbles in the glass, a sign that the glass was handblown. This was a standard practice up until the early 20th century.

Also, it can be as simple as buying the motifs you love. For Widener, it’s country scenes and images of animals. He’s also not put off if they don’t have a frame at all.

“If you aren’t heading to Jaipur anytime soon, then Etsy and eBay are great places to look,” Holasz shares. “Sellers restock weekly. I’d browse quite a few listings before choosing, because the frame can make such a difference. They can sell pretty quicky as they are always in demand though, so it’s worth doing some research to shortlist styles you like so you can move quickly when the right thing comes along.”

If you’re searching for the truly antique varieties (made in the 18th and 19th centuries), Holasz adds that these are likely more available at auctions.

Also, don’t count out flea markets and antique shops for these finds. For reverse glass paintings, according to current eBay listings, you can expect to pay as low as $5 all the way up to $5,000. The prices for these paintings tend to be a steal these days since they’re just starting to have a moment, so snatch them up before the prices rise.

While you can certainly give the spotlight to a single reverse glass painting, many designers believe these diminutive pieces look best when hung together in small groups.

As for rooms where these works look their best, Holasz thinks they look “really adorable” in children’s rooms, from nurseries to bedrooms to playrooms. They also act as hardy artwork that can be displayed in kitchens and baths, since, as Lissack says, the paintings are “protected by being on the rear side of the glass.”

Also, don’t be afraid to go a bit rogue with these pieces. For example, Holasz likes to mix them into a gallery wall alongside oil paintings, drawings, and sketches, since the different surfaces create visual interest. And you don’t have to hang them on the wall—they also look great leaned on bookshelves.

Widener, who often scores unframed versions, prefers to display them on wire plate hangers.

“When placed in a window, the light comes through in a really beautiful way,” he says.

Ford’s Frostwarden Bronco Is a One-Off Rescue Vehicle with 1200 HP

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The Frostwarden blends Ford’s new Megazilla 2.0 crate engine with an extreme search-and-rescue Bronco build.

A base Ford Bronco makes 300 horsepower. Opt for the V-6 option, and it’ll add 30 horses; if that’s not enough, the Bronco Raptor reaches 418. Not there yet? Try doubling the Raptor’s output and adding a few hundred more horsepower. Enter the Megazilla 2.0.

Ford’s most powerful crate engine ever is now on display for the first time at this year’s SEMA show in Las Vegas, Nevada. The Megazilla 2.0, which builds off the 7.3-liter V-8 crate engine lineup, uses a stronger block, upgraded pistons, larger fuel injectors, a different belt drive and damper, and a supercharger-specific camshaft to keep up with a 3.0-liter Gen 6 Whipple supercharger. All of that combines to make over 1200 horsepower and over 1000 pound-feet of torque.

See all results for new 2027 Ford Bronco for sale near 18510

To show off the engine’s massive output, Ford developed the Frostwarden: a search-and-rescue-inspired Bronco taken to extremes. The outfitted Bronco is lifted with a 74Weld portal lift kit and wears 42-inch-tall BFGoodrich tires mounted on custom 18-inch Weld beadlock wheels. Dana driveline hardware and custom Bilstein dampers round out the undercarriage upgrades.

The Frostwarden’s black-and-yellow livery, ultra-wide stance, and plethora of rescue tools—including off-road lights, a rear-mounted external gas tank, roof-mounted spare tire, and bright yellow front tow winch—make the Bronco hard to miss, even among the ranks of modded SEMA show cars. Inside, yellow-and-black upholstery lines the cabin and a special Megazilla wordmark rests along the bottom of the steering wheel.

The Frostwarden is a one-of-a-kind build, but the 7.3-liter supercharged V-8 that powers it isn’t. Ford says the one-off vehicle highlights the ability to launch extreme builds based around Ford-powered engines, including the massive Megazilla 2.0.

In true Hoosier fashion, her first car was a hot pink custom cardboard "Fiero-vette" for her kindergarten Kindy 500 race. She now autocrosses a 1994 Miata with her dad, which she learned to drive the night before her first event. Alayna went to Indiana University and graduated with a degree in journalism and a minor in graphic design. As a testament to her great taste in cars, her first real one was a metallic orange 2007 Chevy HHR, which she lovingly called "The Dumptruck."

Windows 11 8GB RAM configurations get a performance boost

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Microsoft's claimed performance gains will need to be tested

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Following a resurgence of 8GB RAM laptops earlier this year, and in the wake of promises to improve Windows 11 performance using just 8GB of RAM, Microsoft has today announced what it claims are a series of performance improvements. Driven by an ongoing global PC component shortage that is forcing manufacturers to dumb down specs, the company says that with 8GB of RAM, Windows is now 20% more responsive and 40% faster when it comes to multitasking.

The development shared today by Microsoft is an update to a commitment earlier this year to improve the quality and performance of Windows 11, a major bugbear of users since its launch. Microsoft says it has taken a three-pronged approach to memory optimization: making better use of memory in Windows 11 PCs, protecting interactions that need to feel instant, and ensuring key apps and Windows use less memory. While the company admits you'll be able to do more on 16GB or 32GB of RAM, it says "we're making sure you can do more, even with 8GB."

The headline performance gain is a claim that Windows is now up to 20% more responsive on 8GB PCs. Likewise, multitasking is now purportedly 40% faster in Microsoft's lab tests. Of course, these are internal tests conducted in favorable conditions by Microsoft.

Microsoft says it has tuned compression for useful apps and data, and can now keep up to 2GB more of your active data in memory, rather than moving it to storage. In practice, this should mean that apps and tabs left open should be less likely to reload when you go back to them.

The claim of 20% more responsiveness specifically relates to tasks like opening Start, searching, right-clicking, opening Settings, using the Snipping Tool, and opening File Explorer. The company says it has achieved this by backing off background tasks like indexing while you're using your PC actively. Edge now reportedly opens up to 33% faster. There's also an Efficiency Mode for Microsoft Teams, which can be set to on by default, improving launch times by 20%.

Microsoft says that its claims of 40% speed boosts to multitasking are based on a demanding workflow of 20 Microsoft Edge Tabs, a 4K video, large Word, PowerPoint, and Excel files, Outlook, and File Explorer all being opened one after the other.

Other improvements include reducing committed-memory growth by up to 900 MB in apps like Microsoft Edge and 365 apps, and reducing the memory footprint of Word, PowerPoint, Excel, and Outlook by 360MB. Third-party apps can also benefit from improved memory management, with Microsoft stating WhatsApp is already taking advantage.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Moving forward, Microsoft says it has plans to make memory compression even smarter and more efficient. Windows features and apps will share common parts, such as Search and Widgets using one web Engine, for instance. WinUI 3 apps like File Explorer and Notepad will be designed to use less memory and open faster, and Microsoft is working with PC makers and silicon manufacturers to reduce how much memory a PC needs to hold in reserve to support its hardware, making more space for Windows.

Game of the Month: Phantom Blade Zero (October 2026)

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Update [Fri 6th Nov 2026, 6pm]: October offered up a bumper Game of the Month vote thanks to a truly stacked release schedule on PS5 — but as always, only one title can win our prestigious award.

And this time around, that title is Phantom Blade Zero. The debut action game from rapidly emerging Chinese studio S-GAME stole the show by some distance.

It's important to note that it was up against more than 20 other PS5 releases — the most any winner's had to overcome in any of our Game of the Month polls to date.

Over 1,100 votes were cast by readers of Push Square, and these were the top five best PS5 games of October 2026, according to you lot:

Phantom Blade Zero had only been out for a handful of days when voting opened, which just goes to show the kind of immediate impact that it's managed to have.

There's no doubt it's a Game of the Year contender, given review scores and overall popularity.

And it's a good action title, too — a damn good one. Impressive in its scope and ambition, it's a seriously meaty wuxia-style romp, packing top class combat and some surprisingly great RPG elements, like being able to bolster your style of play through a huge selection of perk-based weapons and accessories.

Liam dubbed it "one of the generation's greatest action experiences" in our Phantom Blade Zero PS5 review, blessing it with an 'excellent' 9/10 score.

Review Phantom Blade Zero (PS5) - One of the Generation's Greatest Action Experiences

Congratulations, Soul! You may only have 66 days to live, but this particular victory will be remembered forever.

Original Story: Ah, October, otherwise known as the month before GTA 6.

Indeed, it was an incredibly busy 31 days for new PS5 releases, following on from an equally manic September — and that's going to make this latest Game of the Month poll all the more intriguing.

October's most high profile title arguably launched just a few days ago: Phantom Blade Zero, the PS5 console exclusive that had been wowing onlookers for years with its gameplay trailers, finally arrived on the 29th.

The Chinese action game earned extremely impressive review scores, and it seems to have been a big hit with fans of the genre.

Meanwhile, Call of Duty: Modern Warfare 4 actually managed to deliver what many are calling the series' best campaign in years, and Castlevania: Belmont's Curse revived Konami's iconic franchise in some style.

Other standouts included the eye-watering Star Wars: Galactic Racer — a fantastic racer that's gone down far better than anyone thought it would — and Final Fantasy Resonance, which turned out to be a brilliant callback to the property's traditional JRPG roots.

Ace Combat 8: Wings of Theve deserves a shoutout too; another super highly rated release, and quite possibly the most engaging Ace Combat game of the series' modern era.

Then you've got the more divisive stuff that some of you are bound to vote for: both Stupid Never Dies and Hellraiser: Revival struggled to capture the hearts of critics, but they're the kind of games where if you enjoy them, you probably really enjoy them.

But as always, only one of these titles can be crowned Game of the Month. Before we get to the all-important vote, though, here's a quick recap of our 2026 winners so far...

Now then, what was your PS5 Game of the Month for October 2026? Cast your decisive vote in our poll, and then explain yourself in the comments section below.

Voting closes on Thursday the 5th November 2026 at 00:00 BST.

Air Canada Aeroplan — Partner Awards with Stopovers

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The only major program offering free stopovers on one-way partner awards

## Flying Business Class Across Two Continents for the Price of One Imagine settling into a fully flat bed on a Boeing 787-9 somewhere over the Pacific, a warm towel in your hand, Japan behind you and Singapore ahead. You've already spent three days eating ramen in Tokyo — a stopover you added to your award ticket for a mere 5,000 extra points. This isn't a once-in-a-lifetime splurge. This is what the ChooseFI community calls a **nearly free** trip, engineered through one of the most underappreciated sweet spots in the entire travel rewards ecosystem. The Air Canada Aeroplan stopover rule is genuinely one of the last great loopholes in loyalty travel. Here's the math that makes your jaw drop: a business class flight from Toronto to Singapore — routed through Tokyo with a multi-day stopover — retails for **$8,000 to $10,000 in cash**. You can book that same itinerary for **70,000 Aeroplan points** (plus 5,000 for the stopover, so 75,000 total). At a conservative cash value of $8,000, you're extracting **10.7 cents per point** — more than double what most programs offer, and squarely within Aeroplan's documented range of **5.7 to 14.3 cents per point**. To put that in FI terms: if you value your points at even the low end of that range — 5.7 cents per point — 75,000 Aeroplan points are worth **$4,275 in travel**. At the high end, you're looking at **$10,725**. That's a portfolio-level return on the credit card spending and transfer partners you've already been accumulating. What makes this feel different from other premium redemptions is the *shape* of the trip itself. You're not just flying business class — you're building a mini round-the-world itinerary. Tokyo and Singapore in the same award ticket. Two of the world's great cities. Two business class flights. One redemption. The inefficiency here isn't accidental — it's baked into how Aeroplan prices stopovers at a flat 5,000 miles, regardless of where you stop. The program is essentially inviting you to stack experiences on a single award, and the ChooseFI community is the group paying close enough attention to accept that invitation.

The core insight is this: Air Canada Aeroplan uses a fixed partner award chart for Star Alliance flights, and it prices stopovers at a flat 5,000-mile premium regardless of routing complexity. That single policy decision transforms a standard business class award into a multi-destination adventure at a fraction of the combined retail cost.

Here's the mechanism. When Aeroplan relaunched in 2020, it did something most programs had abandoned years earlier: it kept a fixed-rate award chart for partner airlines. While programs like United MileagePlus moved to fully dynamic pricing — where award costs fluctuate with demand — Aeroplan locked in predictable rates for Star Alliance partners. The YYZ to SIN corridor in business class sits at a rate that reflects the geography, not the current demand spike or revenue management algorithm. That's the pricing inefficiency the ChooseFI community can systematically exploit.

Layer on top of that the stopover rule: Aeroplan allows one free stopover on one-way partner awards for just 5,000 additional miles. A stopover is defined as a connection of more than 24 hours. So your Toronto → Tokyo → Singapore routing becomes a Toronto → Tokyo (stopover 3 days) → Singapore itinerary, all on one award ticket, for 75,000 points total. You've essentially purchased two separate international business class flights for the price of one, plus a nominal surcharge.

The other critical piece: Aeroplan charges zero fuel surcharges on partner awards. This was a deliberate policy decision in the 2020 relaunch and it's a massive advantage. Programs like British Airways Avios and Singapore KrisFlyer can add $500–$1,500 in surcharges to long-haul business class awards. With Aeroplan, you pay government taxes only — typically $50–$150 on most itineraries.

You don't need to fly Air Canada or hold an Air Canada credit card to access this. Aeroplan accepts transfers from several major flexible currency programs at a 1:1 ratio:

Chase Ultimate Rewards is your best friend here because transfers are instant, which matters when you're racing to book a specific award seat before someone else claims it. If you hold a Chase Sapphire Preferred, Chase Sapphire Reserve, or Ink Business Preferred, your UR points can become Aeroplan points in seconds.

The booking window is 355 days out, which gives you nearly a full year of planning runway — a significant advantage for families coordinating schedules or travelers targeting peak periods in Tokyo or Singapore. Partner award availability on Aeroplan is generally solid for the Star Alliance network, with business class seats on ANA, Japan Airlines (where applicable), and Singapore Airlines routes opening up regularly. Availability scores a 4 out of 5 for this sweet spot, which reflects genuine accessibility rather than theoretical possibility.

Free calculator shows when you could reach financial independence — plus a savings rate tracker, expense audit, and more.

Never transfer points until you have confirmed, bookable award space in hand. Go to aeroplan.com and use the flight search tool. Select "Business" class, enter your origin and destination (e.g., Toronto YYZ to Singapore SIN), and search for one-way awards. Aeroplan's search engine will show you available partner space directly — you don't need a separate search on partner airline websites, though checking ana.co.jp or singaporeair.com for business class availability can help you triangulate what's open before logging into Aeroplan.

Look for awards showing Star Alliance partner carriers (ANA, Singapore Airlines, and others depending on routing). The system will display the points cost and any routing options. Aeroplan's booking window opens 355 days in advance, so set a calendar reminder to search the moment your target travel window becomes available.

Once you've found an outbound flight you like (e.g., Toronto → Tokyo), call Aeroplan at 1-800-361-5373 or use the online multi-city search to add a stopover at your midpoint city. A stopover is any connection exceeding 24 hours. The system will add 5,000 points to your base award cost. Confirm the full itinerary shows both the Tokyo stopover segment and the onward Tokyo → Singapore flight in the cabin class you want.

Note: Online booking for complex multi-city awards with stopovers sometimes requires a phone call to complete. Budget 30–45 minutes for the call and have your routing clearly written out before you dial.

Determine which flexible currency you'll use. If you have Chase Ultimate Rewards, transfer to Aeroplan first — it's instant. Log into your Chase account, navigate to "Transfer to Travel Partners," select Air Canada Aeroplan, and complete the transfer. Points appear in your Aeroplan account immediately.

If using Amex Membership Rewards, Capital One, or Bilt, initiate the transfer and expect a 1–2 day processing window. Have your Aeroplan member number ready. Do NOT transfer until you've confirmed availability in Step 1.

Once your points land in your Aeroplan account, return to aeroplan.com and complete the booking within the same browser session if possible. Pay the government taxes and fees with a credit card that earns rewards on travel purchases. Taxes on a routing like YYZ → NRT → SIN are typically in the $100–$200 range — no fuel surcharges.

After booking, download or screenshot your itinerary confirmation. Verify the cabin class on each segment. Check in on the Aeroplan website 24 hours before each flight and confirm your seat assignments. For the stopover city, you are responsible for your own accommodation — budget accordingly, and consider this the best "forced layover" you'll ever take.

A stopover is a deliberate break in your journey at a connecting city, where you stay for more than 24 hours before continuing to your final destination. Most loyalty programs treat stopovers as separate tickets, requiring you to pay miles for each segment independently. Aeroplan breaks this convention by allowing one free stopover on one-way awards, meaning your connecting city becomes a bonus destination at zero additional mileage cost. For example, a flight from New York to Tokyo with a 5-day stopover in Istanbul costs the same 75,000 Aeroplan points as flying directly — but you get to explore Istanbul for nearly a week as part of the trip. The stopover must be at a connection point that makes geographic sense for your routing, but with 26+ Star Alliance airlines and hundreds of hub cities, the options are vast.

Let's build out a specific redemption so the math becomes undeniable.

The Itinerary: You depart Toronto Pearson (YYZ) on an ANA Boeing 787-9 bound for Tokyo Narita (NRT). You've built in a 3-day stopover in Tokyo — enough time for a morning at Tsukiji, an evening in Shinjuku, and a day trip to Nikko. Then you board a Singapore Airlines flight from NRT to Singapore Changi (SIN) in Business Class.

The Cash Price: A business class itinerary covering this exact routing — YYZ to NRT to SIN — retails in the range of $8,000 to $10,000, depending on season and carrier. Booking two separate one-way tickets (YYZ-NRT and NRT-SIN) in business class would easily exceed that, often landing closer to $10,000–$12,000 combined.

The Points Cost: 70,000 Aeroplan points for the base award + 5,000 for the stopover = 75,000 points total, plus approximately $120–$180 in government taxes. No fuel surcharges.

The CPP Calculation: At a midpoint cash value of $9,000, your 75,000 points are generating 12 cents per point. Even at the conservative $8,000 end, you're at 10.7 cents per point — well within the documented 5.7–14.3 cent range and significantly above the ChooseFI rule of thumb of 2 cents per point for most redemptions.

On the ANA 787-9 YYZ–NRT segment, you're in ANA's Business Staggered seat — a fully flat bed with direct aisle access, Japanese hospitality, and one of the best business class dining experiences in the sky. The Tokyo stopover is yours to explore however you like. Then Singapore Airlines' Business Class on the NRT–SIN leg offers its own benchmark product — a refined seat, excellent service, and arrival into Changi, consistently ranked the world's best airport.

You've visited two of Asia's most celebrated cities, flown on two world-class airlines, slept flat across the Pacific, and your out-of-pocket cash cost was under $200. That is the ChooseFI travel rewards mindset in its purest form.

Tokyo in cherry blossom season (late March to early April) and autumn foliage season (November) books up incredibly fast — even in business class. Set a calendar reminder for exactly 355 days before your target departure and search the moment Aeroplan's window opens. Partner award space on popular routes gets claimed quickly.

Because Chase Ultimate Rewards transfers to Aeroplan instantly, you can hold an award seat without pre-positioning points in your account. Find the space first, then transfer exactly what you need. This is a significant tactical advantage over programs that require 2–5 day transfer windows.

Aeroplan allows open jaw routing. You can fly INTO Tokyo and OUT of Singapore on separate awards, pairing this business class redemption with a separate economy or business award home. Build your Southeast Asia trip so you land in one city and depart from another — no backtracking, better geography, same award pricing framework.

This is the single most common mistake in all of travel rewards. If you transfer 75,000 Chase UR to Aeroplan and then discover the award space disappeared, you cannot transfer back. Chase points are gone. Confirm bookable space first, transfer second, book third.

Marriott transfers to Aeroplan at a 3:1 ratio — meaning you need 225,000 Marriott points to generate 75,000 Aeroplan points. That's an extraordinarily poor ratio for this redemption. Use Chase, Amex, Capital One, or Bilt instead.

The Chase Sapphire Reserve earns 3x on travel and dining. The Amex Platinum earns 5x on flights booked directly. Build your Aeroplan balance through the transfer partners rather than flying Air Canada itself — the flexibility is far more valuable.

Unlike many programs that expire your miles after 12–18 months of inactivity, Aeroplan points never expire. This means you can accumulate slowly and deliberately — a natural fit for the FI community's patient, systematic approach to building travel rewards balances.

Free calculators, a personalized FI roadmap, and a community of 25K+ on the same journey.

This redemption is ideal for couples and solo travelers with a genuine interest in Asia — specifically anyone who has ever dreamed of Japan and Southeast Asia in the same trip without paying for two separate premium tickets. If your travel bucket list includes Tokyo and Singapore in the same itinerary, this is the single most efficient way to get there in business class.

Minimum points needed: ~75,000 Aeroplan points (70,000 base + 5,000 stopover). Build this through Chase UR, Amex MR, Capital One, or Bilt transfers.

For the FI-minded traveler, this is a high-ROI, low-cash-cost adventure that fits perfectly into a strategy of saving aggressively while experiencing the world nearly free.

## Why This Is Rated Moderate — And How to Handle It This sweet spot earns a **moderate difficulty** rating — not because the math is complicated, but because the booking process has more moving parts than a simple domestic award. **What makes it moderate:** 1. **Complex routing with a stopover** — Aeroplan's online tool doesn't always handle multi-city stopover bookings cleanly. You may need to call Aeroplan to finalize the itinerary, which adds a step and requires you to have your routing clearly planned before you dial. 2. **Partner award availability** — You're booking seats on ANA, Singapore Airlines, or other Star Alliance carriers, not Air Canada. Partner space is real and accessible (this scores a 4/5 for availability), but it requires searching specifically for it on aeroplan.com and sometimes triangulating with partner airline websites. 3. **Points transfer timing** — You need points in your Aeroplan account before completing the booking, which means coordinating a transfer after confirming space exists. Chase's instant transfer largely solves this problem. **Three tips to make it manageable:** - **Use aeroplan.com's multi-city search first** to understand what routing options exist before calling. Come to any phone call with a primary and backup itinerary already researched. - **Book 6–12 months out** using the 355-day window. More availability, more flexibility, less stress. - **Do one transfer before you need to** — move a small amount of points to Aeroplan just to practice the process and verify your accounts are linked. If you've successfully booked a domestic business class award before, this is a natural next step. The complexity is manageable, and the reward — business class across two ocean crossings with a Tokyo stopover — is absolutely worth the extra 45 minutes of work.

Identify your final destination and choose a stopover city that lies along a logical routing. Popular stopover cities include Istanbul (Turkish Airlines hub), Singapore (Singapore Airlines hub), Tokyo (ANA hub), Frankfurt (Lufthansa hub), and Zurich (Swiss hub). The stopover city must be a connection point that Aeroplan considers valid for your route.

On aircanada.com, use the multi-city search option. Enter your origin to the stopover city as the first segment, and the stopover city to your final destination as the second segment. Set business class and search. The system will show you combined availability and pricing with the stopover included.

Once you confirm availability, transfer the required points from Chase Ultimate Rewards (instant to 24 hours), Amex Membership Rewards (1-3 days), Capital One (1-2 days), or Bilt (1-2 days). Transfer only after confirming both segments have availability.

Follow the booking flow on aircanada.com, selecting both segments. The system will recognize the stopover and price it as a single one-way award. Confirm the total mileage cost matches the one-way rate for your destination — the stopover should add zero additional miles.

With up to 45 days at your stopover city, plan accommodations and activities. Consider using hotel points for your stopover stay — Hyatt points in Istanbul or IHG points in Singapore pair well with an Aeroplan stopover. The stopover is the bonus trip, so treat it as a mini-vacation within your larger journey.

Aeroplan allows stopovers of up to 45 days on one-way awards. This is more than enough time for a genuine visit to your stopover city. You can stay as briefly as 24 hours (anything less is considered a connection, not a stopover) or as long as 45 days before continuing to your final destination.

No. The free stopover on a one-way award is included at no additional mileage cost. Your total point cost is determined by the origin-to-final-destination pricing, not by the number of stops along the way. A flight from New York to Bangkok with a stopover in Istanbul costs the same as New York to Bangkok direct.

Aeroplan prices round trips as two one-way tickets. Each one-way award allows one free stopover, so a round-trip booking can include up to two stopovers — one on the outbound and one on the return. This effectively turns a round trip into a four-destination itinerary.

Lufthansa, Swiss, Austrian, and THAI Airways impose significant fuel surcharges on award tickets that can add $100 to $400 per segment. To minimize out-of-pocket costs, route through carriers with no or low surcharges: Turkish Airlines, ANA, EVA Air, Singapore Airlines (on most routes), and United. Check the fare breakdown before confirming your booking.

Aeroplan's free stopover on one-way awards is a genuinely unique feature that no other major North American loyalty program offers. The ability to spend a week in Istanbul, Singapore, or Tokyo as a free add-on to your business class award makes Aeroplan one of the most valuable programs in the travel rewards ecosystem. Combine this with access to 26+ Star Alliance carriers, online booking for stopover routings, and 1:1 transfers from Chase, Amex, Capital One, and Bilt, and you have a program that delivers outsized value for the ChooseFI community. Build a transferable points balance, identify your dream two-destination trip, and let Aeroplan's stopover rule turn one award into two vacations.

CM: Dalot could reunite with Amorim at Milan as Man Utd deal winds down

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Diogo Dalot has once again been linked with a potential return to AC Milan, where he spent the 2020-21 season on loan.

Calciomercato.com report that Roma’s visit to face Manchester United at Old Trafford will not only be an opportunity for the Giallorossi to face and perhaps erase the ghosts of the past, but also to watch someone up close who could be a name for the not-too-distant future: Diogo Dalot.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

The Portuguese full-back’s contract is expiring at the end of next season and although he is happy in Manchester, many clubs are beginning to ask his entourage for details.

Roma were one step away from landing the defender in the summer of 2021 when the Portuguese coach José Mourinho was on the bench. The negotiations dragged on for a long time, but in the end it didn’t go through.

The reason ended up being quite simple: the Red Devils never agreed to a loan with option to buy formula while Roma – who were still severely limited by Financial Fair Play limitations – could not offer anything more.

Dalot knows Italy and Serie A well, and he recently spoke about his loan experience with Milan in an interview with The Player’s Tribune.

“It was one of the most important years of my career. I remember the way the club welcomed me and how welcome I felt in a year that was important to me, one in which I needed to regain my confidence in my football. Milan helped me get back to those levels,” he said.

“Every day, Paolo Maldini was on the sidelines to watch us train. Rain, shine, or cold, he was always there, in a suit and tie. He would say to me: ‘Mark like this’ or ‘Tackle it.’ Other times, he would scold me for trying too hard. ‘You’re an extraordinary player, don’t worry, you’ll see, everything will be fine’.”

As mentioned, Dalot’s deal expires in 2028 but there is the presence of a one-year clause that United can trigger. There are a number of teams monitoring his situation including Inter, Milan and Juventus, who have all enquired in recent weeks.

Nothing concrete has emerged yet, as Dalot happy in Manchester and isn’t thinking about the future. However, if things don’t change, a departure – perhaps even for a low-cost fee – could become a realistic possibility.

With the Rossoneri needing defenders, could they try pounce on a reunion for a versatile player that they and Ruben Amorim know well?

American Airlines Passenger Stuck Overnight Couldn’t Get A Hotel Voucher—They Got A Credit Card Pitch Instead

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

An American Airlines passenger says they couldn’t get a hotel voucher during a flight delay. The chat agent did, however, have something else ready: a pitch to open a Citi credit card, spend $2,500, and earn 50,000 miles. That’s not even as good as the standard offer! American’s stated purpose is “To Care for People on Life’s Journey.” That journey includes a sales presentation while you’re trying to figure out where to sleep. The passenger shared a screenshot of the exchange. It begins with the agent asking, “Does any of that work for you?” Then comes a lengthy offer for the Citi / AAdvantage Platinum Select Mastercard: the initial bonus, free checked bags, double miles at restaurants and gas stations, and a request for permission to send a one-time email with more information. American Airlines offered me a credit card deal during a conversation about a delayed flight and their inability to provide a hotel voucher from mildlyinfuriating Always be selling! Always be closing! It’s Glengarry Glen Ross, and the passenger asking for help is the lead. American Pays Agents To Make These Pitches In May 2023, I reported that American’s reservations agents would switch from pitching Avis rental cars to Citi credit cards. At the time, I argued that agents should judge whether the conversation had gone well enough for a customer to be receptive. American’s AAdvantage Cash support materials explain why an agent might make the ask either way: Contact center and Premium Guest Services representatives earn at least $50 for each approved application carrying their referral code. That rises to $100 as they move through annual sales tiers. There are also $100, $300, and $500 bonuses for reaching Gold, Platinum, and Diamond. They earn elite status like we do. There’s also a separate guide to pitching cards during chats, complete with prescribed transitions from talking about travel benefits to offering the card. American Already Knows When To Stop Selling I’ve written for years about credit card announcements that interrupt sleep on early morning flights. As Kevin Williamson once put it, [T]here is the Clockwork Orange sensation of being literally strapped into a seat while someone screams corporate banalities at you on a loudspeaker fifteen inches from your head… It is a “limited-time” offer in the sense that the sun eventually will run out of gas and become a dying star, first engulfing the Earth in fire and then leaving the wreck of the solar system a sterile plane of interstellar cold and utter silence, which will be interrupted only by some addled flight attendant screeching about the limited-time Barclays card offer. The airline’s guidelines at least suggest: Use the approved script, which should take about a minute. Make the announcement only once per flight segment, at cruise before descent, between 8 a.m. and 9 p.m. Skip late night and redeye flights. Refrain from offering the card or making announcements during delays, cancellations, diversions, and other events affecting the customer experience. Meanwhile, the pressure to sell keeps growing. On October 15, the flight attendants union objected to American inserting a 15-minute card-sales module into mandatory fourth quarter training. Selling remains voluntary, but management made the training required and offered a $25 qualification reward. They Have A Sales Script For Your Bag Fee, Too American’s airport agent talking points for “Real Time Benefits” identify two moments to pitch: when a customer is about to pay a checked bag fee, and when they’re worried there won’t be overhead bin space for their carry-on. For most instantly approved applicants, the card’s first checked bag benefit and Group 5 boarding can apply to the trip a passenger is already taking. The airline says the boarding group can take roughly seven minutes to update on a digital boarding pass.
